USER: I'm on phase 3. Syncthing on the server is the Docker version with `/home/user/GitHub` mapped to `/Github` in the compose file (the client has the `dnf` version). With these settings:
```xml
    <folder id="redacted" label="Project (LineByLine)" path="/GitHub/linebyline" type="sendreceive" rescanIntervalS="3600" fsWatcherEnabled="true" fsWatcherDelayS="1" fsWatcherTimeoutS="0" ignorePerms="false" autoNormalize="true">
```
It took ~11.79s to create a `blahblah.txt` file in Dolphin (/home/user/GitHub/linebyline) on the client and watch it appear in Dolphin (same path) on the server (it has a full KDE environment, like the client). And ~10.00s for a deletion on client to be appear on the server. Should I reduce `rescanIntervalS` too?

ASSISTANT: **No** — `rescanIntervalS` is a fallback full-tree walk, not the primary detection path. With `fsWatcherEnabled=true` and `fsWatcherDelayS=1` working, it never fires for normal edits. Reducing it just adds CPU/IO cost; it doesn't accelerate the inotify pipeline.

**The 11s itself is the real problem.** Expected breakdown with your settings (LAN gigabit):
- ~1s: client fsWatcherDelayS batch
- ~0.5s: client hash + send
- ~0.5s: server receive + write through bind-mount
- ~9s: ???

That ~9s is most likely **Dolphin's directory refresh interval on the Server.** KDE's KIO slave doesn't inotify-watch the directory listing by default; it polls. The file probably arrived on the Server's filesystem 2-3s after creation, but Dolphin doesn't re-render the listing until its next tick. The OMP agent's loop is unaffected by this (no Dolphin involved) — only the human's interactive experience is.

**Isolate the actual sync time from Dolphin's render:**
```sh
date +%s.%N; touch ~/GitHub/linebyline/.sync-test; \
  ssh server 'while [ ! -f ~/GitHub/linebyline/.sync-test ]; do sleep 0.05; done; date +%s.%N'; \
  rm ~/GitHub/linebyline/.sync-test
```
Expect ~1-2s. If you see that, the OMP loop's 2s wait (Phase 5) is safe.

**Secondary suspect:** Server-side fsWatcher inside Docker. Bind-mount + inotify through the container is known-flaky; the inotify watch is on the container's filesystem view, not the host's, and depending on the storage driver and kernel version, the kernel can refuse to attach the watch. Check the Server's Syncthing GUI → Folder Status for `linebyline` — it should say **"Watching"**, not **"Polling"** or **"Unable to monitor"**. If it's silently polling (default 10s), that's a separate floor on every change that the `fsWatcherDelayS=1` doesn't help with.

**If the CLI diagnostic shows 5s+,** the Server's fsWatcher in Docker is broken. Cleanest fix is running Syncthing natively on the Server — the handoff says it's an old laptop on your desk, not a container host, and the handoff already established that Podman is the only thing that needs the host kernel directly. If you don't want to unwind the Docker setup, you can set `fsWatcherEnabled=false` on the Server-side folder and reduce `rescanIntervalS` to something like 5–10s — but that's the *only* scenario where reducing `rescanIntervalS` makes sense, and it's a fallback for a known-broken inotify, not a primary optimization.

The ~175ms SSH warm path and the 2s sync wait are independent of this. The OMP loop's correctness doesn't depend on Dolphin; the handoff's `2s` is fine for the CLI-measured sync.

ASSISTANT: Two problems:

**1. The cleanup `rm` ran before the `ssh` finished** (it used `;`, not `&&`, and `ssh` failed fast on the bad-owner error). So no second date was ever printed, and the timing is garbage.

**2. `ssh Server` is actually broken right now.** The handoff's `ssh -v ... 'echo REACHED'` verification must have filtered out the "bad owner" line via the grep pattern — it's an SSH client error, not a connection success or failure indicator, but it does cause SSH to abort. This wasn't a problem at handoff time, or it would have been visible then.

The bad owner is on a symlink in `/etc/ssh/ssh_config.d/`:
- `20-systemd-ssh-proxy.conf` → `../../../usr/lib/systemd/ssh_config.d/20-systemd-ssh-proxy.conf`
- Owned by `nobody:nobody`, mode 777
- Should be `root:root`, mode 644 (or 755 on the dir)

I can't fix it — `/etc` is write-blocked in this sandbox. You need:
```sh
sudo chown root:root /etc/ssh/ssh_config.d/20-systemd-ssh-proxy.conf
sudo chmod 644 /etc/ssh/ssh_config.d/20-systemd-ssh-proxy.conf
```
Or if it's the symlink target that needs the fix (more likely — `/usr` is read-only-ish but the file in `/usr/lib/systemd/ssh_config.d/` should already be root-owned, so probably the symlink itself is the one to fix):
```sh
sudo chown -h root:root /etc/ssh/ssh_config.d/20-systemd-ssh-proxy.conf
sudo chmod 644 /etc/ssh/ssh_config.d/20-systemd-ssh-proxy.conf
```
(`-h` matters — without it, `chown` follows the symlink to the target.)

Then re-run the timing, this time without the cleanup race:

ASSISTANT: Same failure — the `Bad owner or permissions` line is SSH *aborting before* it even reads `~/.ssh/config`, so the `Server` alias never gets resolved. The whole `ssh` block returns immediately, the `while` loop never runs on the remote, and the trailing `rm` cleans up the local file. That's why you see only the first `date` timestamp.

You have two paths:

**Path A — fix the broken system file (the real fix).** `/etc` is sandbox-write-blocked here; you have to run this yourself in a regular terminal:
```sh
sudo chown -h root:root /etc/ssh/ssh_config.d/20-systemd-ssh-proxy.conf
sudo chmod 644 /etc/ssh/ssh_config.d/20-systemd-ssh-proxy.conf
```
After that, `ssh Server` works and the timing test will produce a real number.

**Path B — bypass the system config to get the measurement now (doesn't fix the underlying problem, just lets you measure).** `ssh` reads `/etc/ssh/ssh_config` first, then the files in `ssh_config.d/`, then `~/.ssh/config`. The `bad owner` check is per-file, but `Match` directives can skip files. There's no clean per-file skip — but you can pass `-F /dev/null` to skip *all* system and user config, which means you lose your `Server` alias and must spell it out:
```sh
date +%s.%N; touch ~/GitHub/linebyline/.sync-test; \
  ssh -F /dev/null -i ~/.ssh/id_ed25519_server -o IdentitiesOnly=yes -o AddKeysToAgent=no \
      user@192.168.1.254 \
      'while [ ! -f ~/GitHub/linebyline/.sync-test ]; do sleep 0.05; done; date +%s.%N'; \
  rm -f ~/GitHub/linebyline/.sync-test
```
Same race issue with the trailing `rm` — it runs whether `ssh` succeeds or fails. To make the measurement valid:
```sh
t0=$(date +%s.%N); touch ~/GitHub/linebyline/.sync-test; \
  ssh -F /dev/null -i ~/.ssh/id_ed25519_server -o IdentitiesOnly=yes user@192.168.1.254 \
      'while [ ! -f ~/GitHub/linebyline/.sync-test ]; do sleep 0.05; done'; \
  t1=$(date +%s.%N); echo "elapsed: $(echo "$t1 - $t0" | bc) s"; \
  rm -f ~/GitHub/linebyline/.sync-test
```

**Recommendation:** do Path A. The bad-owner symlink is a real bug in your SSH client config that will bite the OMP agent and any other SSH-using tool (git, rsync, etc.) — `git push` to GitHub might already be going through a different code path that hides this, but anything new will hit it. The fix is one `sudo chown -h` away.

**Test it directly on your real box:**

If the first line is `Bad owner or permissions on /etc/ssh/ssh_config.d/20-systemd-ssh-proxy.conf`, then the alias doesn't work in any tool that reads the system config cascade (which is everything).

If the `Bad owner` error is real on your box, the cause is almost certainly SELinux. SSH refuses to parse any file in `ssh_config.d/` whose security context isn't `etc_t` or isn't readable in the right way. To check:
```sh
ls -lZ /etc/ssh/ssh_config.d/20-systemd-ssh-proxy.conf
sudo matchpathcon /etc/ssh/ssh_config.d/20-systemd-ssh-proxy.conf
```
If the SELinux type is anything other than `etc_t` (e.g. `home_t` because something set it to a user-home context, or unlabeled), that's the culprit. Fix:
```sh
sudo restorecon -v /etc/ssh/ssh_config.d/20-systemd-ssh-proxy.conf
```

If that doesn't fix it, the next-most-likely cause is the `Match` directive in the file evaluating weirdly. Quick workaround:
```sh
sudo mv /etc/ssh/ssh_config.d/20-systemd-ssh-proxy.conf /etc/ssh/ssh_config.d/20-systemd-ssh-proxy.conf.disabled
```
— disables the file entirely. The systemd-ssh-proxy feature (connecting to `machine/*`, `unix/*`, `.host`) will stop working, but you don't use it. Your `Server` alias then loads directly from `~/.ssh/config`.

Run the first `ssh -v` test and tell me what you see.

ASSISTANT: **Found it.** The symlink has type `etc_t` (correct), but the **target** has type `systemd_conf_t`. SSH's config-parsing check evaluates the resolved file, not the symlink itself. The strict policy path on Fedora 44 is rejecting `systemd_conf_t` in `ssh_config.d/`. (`systemd_conf_t` is fine for files in `/etc/systemd/`, but apparently not in `/etc/ssh/ssh_config.d/`.)

**Fix it without root on `/usr`:** relabel just the symlink's target by relabeling the symlink itself, or by re-symlinking to a different path. Cleanest: **copy** the file and change the symlink:

```sh
sudo cp /usr/lib/systemd/ssh_config.d/20-systemd-ssh-proxy.conf \
        /etc/ssh/ssh_config.d/20-systemd-ssh-proxy.conf
sudo rm /etc/ssh/ssh_config.d/20-systemd-ssh-proxy.conf
# (cp followed by rm of the symlink leaves the new file in place; the cp preserves the symlink's basename, so the file ends up at the right path)
# If SELinux was the problem, the new file gets whatever context cp assigns (probably unconfined_u or machine_t). Force it:
sudo chcon -t etc_t /etc/ssh/ssh_config.d/20-systemd-ssh-proxy.conf
# Or, if restorecon is available and policy says "etc_t for /etc/ssh/ssh_config.d/.*":
sudo restorecon -v /etc/ssh/ssh_config.d/20-systemd-ssh-proxy.conf
```

Then verify:
```sh
ssh -v Server 'echo REACHED' 2>&1 | grep -E 'Bad owner|REACHED|Authenticated'
```
Expect `REACHED` and no `Bad owner`.

**Alternative (the one I'd actually use):** just disable the proxy file — you don't use `machine/*`, `unix/*`, `vsock/*`, or `.host`:
```sh
sudo mv /etc/ssh/ssh_config.d/20-systemd-ssh-proxy.conf /etc/ssh/ssh_config.d/20-systemd-ssh-proxy.conf.disabled
```
Zero behavioral impact, no copy, no SELinux games. If you ever do need the systemd SSH proxy for `.host` or `machine/*` connections, undo the rename.

I'd go with the `.disabled` rename. One line, reversible, and avoids any future interaction with the file's contents.

**On the KWallet/passphrase question:** unrelated to this. The handoff confirms `kwallet-ssh-agent` + `ksshaskpass` autostart wires up the `id_ed25519_server` passphrase at login. If it's not working in this fresh-session state, that's a Phase 5 concern (the OMP agent would also fail). But the immediate `Bad owner` error is what's killing `ssh Server` *before* it ever gets to authentication — fix that first, then verify auth works:
```sh
ssh Server 'echo REACHED'   # after the fix; should print REACHED with no prompt
```

ASSISTANT: **7.06 seconds for the file to materialize on the Server's filesystem.** That's a real Syncthing delay, not a Dolphin render artifact. Dolphin's polling is on top of this, which is why your manual stopwatch saw 11s.

**Why 7s, not ~1s** (with `fsWatcherEnabled=true` and `fsWatcherDelayS=1`):

1. **Docker-inotify is the most likely cause.** The Server's Syncthing runs in a container with `~/GitHub` bind-mounted to `/Github` in the compose. The kernel's inotify subsystem requires the watcher to be attached to the actual inode being modified, and bind mounts + overlayfs frequently break that attachment silently. Syncthing then falls back to its periodic scanner, and the rescan interval on this folder is `3600` — but wait, 7s is too fast for a 1h rescan. The fallback is the **folder-level "not watching" warning**, which triggers a short internal polling loop (~5-10s on average).

2. **Check the Server's Syncthing GUI** for the `linebyline` folder status. It will say one of:
   - `Local Additions` / `Syncing` / `Up to Date` — and below the path, `Watching` or `Polling` or `Unable to monitor`. If it says `Polling`, inotify is broken in the container and you're getting the 5-10s polling floor.
   - A specific error about `fsWatcher` not being supported.

**To confirm:** from the Server, run:
```sh
ssh Server 'cat /proc/1/cgroup 2>/dev/null | head -3; echo ---; docker exec <syncthing-container> cat /proc/sys/fs/inotify/max_user_watches 2>/dev/null'
```
Or via the Syncthing API:
```sh
ssh Server 'curl -s -H "X-API-Key: $(docker exec <syncthing-container> cat /var/syncthing/config/cert.pem 2>/dev/null | head -1)" http://localhost:8384/rest/system/status' 2>/dev/null
```

**Fix options, in order of intrusiveness:**

A. **Set `fsWatcherEnabled=false` and `rescanIntervalS=5`** on the folder. The polling floor you have is already ~5-10s; making it explicit at 5s is honest, deterministic, and the OMP 2s wait becomes 5s. Simple.

B. **Mount the host's `~/GitHub` as a `Bind` (not a `bind propagation: slave` or default `rprivate`) with `:rshared` or `:rslave`** in the compose. This is the technically correct fix for inotify to work through the bind. Verify with `findmnt` inside the container — the mount options should include `shared:` or `slave:`. Documentation: <https://docs.docker.com/storage/bind-mounts/#configure-bind-propagation>.

C. **Run Syncthing natively on the Server**, alongside the Docker version or replacing it. The handoff already noted the Server is a laptop on the desk, not a container host. But you might want Syncthing-in-Docker for other folders or for the GUI-from-other-machines convenience.

**My recommendation:** A. The 5s wait is fine — even with 7s measured, it's bounded and predictable. Adding it as a constant to the OMP loop (Phase 5 instruction: "wait at least 7s for Syncthing to mirror") is fine. Going down the bind-propagation rabbit hole to save 5s on a workflow that's already 5-30s dominated by Podman startup is bad cost/benefit. If the OMP agent's rerun loop becomes the bottleneck later, revisit C.

ASSISTANT: **No, `ssh 192.168.1.254` from the Server is fine** — it just round-trips through the LAN to itself, which exercises the same SSH code path the client uses (so it's a valid test) and is the only way to run the diagnostic, since your `Server` alias is defined on the client.

**The TOFU prompt is the real problem.** You haven't connected to the Server's sshd from the Server yet, so `~/.ssh/known_hosts` on the Server has no entry for `192.168.1.254`. The handoff did the host-key check from the **client** (the PC) using `ssh-copy-id`, which added the host key to the **client's** `known_hosts` only. The Server's `known_hosts` is empty for its own IP.

**Two things to do:**

1. **Accept the host key once** (verify the fingerprint matches what you saw at handoff — the SHA256 was already recorded then). Then `ssh 192.168.1.254` from the Server becomes silent.

2. **Or skip the round-trip entirely** — run the inotify check directly on the Server in a local shell (no SSH):
```sh
cat /proc/1/cgroup | head -3
echo ---
podman exec <container-name-or-id> cat /proc/sys/fs/inotify/max_user_watches 2>/dev/null
# or for Docker:
docker exec <container-name-or-id> cat /proc/sys/fs/inotify/max_user_watches 2>/dev/null
```
You said the Server uses Docker Compose. The container name should be something like `syncthing` (matching the service name in `compose.yaml`) or `github_syncthing_1` (compose default). Find it with:
```sh
docker ps --format '{{.Names}}\t{{.Image}}\t{{.Status}}' | grep -i sync
```

The cgroup info will tell us if the host is cgroup v2 (almost certainly on Fedora 44) — relevant for whether inotify works the same way through the bind mount.

**Why I suggested SSH originally:** if you ran the check from the client, you'd see the *client's* view of the Server's filesystem, which is what matters for the OMP loop. But for inotify diagnostics, we need to see the Server's container's view, which is best observed from the Server directly.

So: type `yes` to the prompt, accept the key, then re-run the diagnostic. Or skip the SSH and run the equivalent `docker exec` from a local Server shell.

ASSISTANT: Two separate things to address:

**1. The `Broken pipe` on `ssh 192.168.1.254` from the Server to itself is real and worth investigating later** — but not blocking now. The connection succeeded far enough to add the key, then died during session setup. Likely causes: `MaxStartups` exhausted, `AllowUsers` not matching (you connected as `user@fedora` from a TTY but Server's `sshd_config` might have an AllowUsers that doesn't match the connecting user — but you said both boxes have the same `user` account, so this should be fine), or `Match` block rejecting the local source. The exit 255 is the connection-killed-by-server-during-handshake. Test from outside (the client) and from the Server with a simple `ssh 192.168.1.254 'whoami'` after a `sleep 5` to rule out rate-limiting.

The handoff's `ssh-copy-id` from the client established that the Server accepts SSH from the client. If Server→Server SSH doesn't work, that's a separate issue and matters for the OMP agent only if it ever needs to test SSH from the Server.

**2. The inotify diagnostic is good. `136464` is plenty of watches** (default is 8192; the inotify limit isn't the problem). The `0::/init.scope` cgroup confirms cgroup v2, as expected on Fedora 44.

**Now the actual investigation:** does Syncthing's fsWatcher work inside the Docker container with the bind mount? Two things to check directly:

```sh
docker exec syncthing ls -la /GitHub/linebyline/.sync-test 2>&1 | head -5
```
(If the test file from your earlier timing is still there, this confirms the bind mount is alive.)

```sh
docker exec syncthing cat /proc/$(pidof syncthing 2>/dev/null || echo 1)/mountinfo 2>/dev/null | grep -i github
```
Look for the bind mount line and check its propagation flags. Expected:
```
... /home/user/GitHub /Github ... rw,...
```
The flags column should include either `shared:N`, `master:N`, or just bare `rw` (private). If it says `master:N` or `shared:N` with N being a peer group ID, propagation is set and inotify *should* work. If it says just `rw` with no propagation flag, the bind is `rprivate` and inotify from inside the container won't see host changes.

**Cleaner check via Syncthing itself:** the Syncthing GUI on the Server (open `http://localhost:8384` in a browser, since it's running on the Server's localhost) → folder status for `linebyline`. It will show one of:
- `Local Additions` / `Syncing` / `Up to Date` — and **the second line under the folder** says either `Watching`, `Polling`, or `Unable to monitor`
- The text `not watching: inotify watch failed` or similar

This is the authoritative answer. If it says `Polling`, you've confirmed Docker-inotify is broken in this setup, and option A (set `rescanIntervalS=5`) is the right move.

**The fastest path:** open the Syncthing GUI in a browser on the Server (or via the client: `ssh -L 8384:localhost:8384 Server`, then `http://localhost:8384`), look at the folder, and tell me what it says. If the GUI is too much, paste the output of the two `docker exec` commands and I'll decode the mount flags.

ASSISTANT: **Confirmed: Docker-inotify is the culprit.**

Evidence from the GUI:
- Folder path: `/GitHub/linebyline` (bind mount target)
- **"Rescans: 1h, Enabled"** with no fsWatcher indicator (the GUI shows fsWatcher status as a separate row or label; absence of it = not watching)
- **"Last Scan: 2026-08-15 22:34:24"** — only updates on a full scan
- **"Latest Change: Deleted .sync-test"** — and the GUI detected it, so Syncthing *is* working, just on the slow path

The 7s you measured is actually the rescan floor, not a delay. The `last scan` happens roughly every 1h, but Syncthing also runs a fast "check if anything changed" loop when the fsWatcher is broken — that's the 5-10s polling you observed. The `.sync-test` file was created at one timestamp, the rescan happened to land ~7s later, found the new file, synced it, and then the GUI logged the *delete* event (which is fast because the delete came in via the polling window too).

**Two fixes, pick one:**

**A. Quick (5 min):** edit the folder's `<fsWatcherEnabled>` to `false` and set `<rescanIntervalS>` to `5` on the Server. The polling floor becomes a deterministic 5s. Bump the OMP agent's wait from 2s to 5s. Zero risk, no compose changes, no Syncthing restart drama.

```sh
docker exec syncthing sed -i 's|<rescanIntervalS>3600</rescanIntervalS>|<rescanIntervalS>5</rescanIntervalS>|; s|<fsWatcherEnabled>true</fsWatcherEnabled>|<fsWatcherEnabled>false</fsWatcherEnabled>|' /var/syncthing/config/config.xml
docker restart syncthing
```

**B. Proper (30 min):** fix the bind mount propagation in the compose file. Add `bind:propagation=rshared` to the `/home/user/GitHub` → `/Github` mount (or move the volume declaration to long syntax: `~/GitHub:/Github:rshared`). Then the inotify watch attaches correctly. Verify with the `mountinfo` check after `docker compose up -d`. The 7s becomes ~1s.

I recommend **A**. The 5s wait is invisible in the OMP loop (Podman startup is 5-30s), and the bind-propagation fix is fragile (depends on Docker version, kernel version, SELinux policy). If a future iteration wants sub-second sync for some reason, revisit B then.

  rm -f ~/GitHub/linebyline/.sync-test
```
Expect ~5s.

ASSISTANT: **13.4s from the client (your original 7s floor is now 13.4s — the client side has its own fsWatcher issues, separate from Docker).** And the Server→Server check is unusable for automation: password prompt + "Permission denied" retries, and you correctly noted you don't want the OMP agent to ever use that path.

But the OMP loop never goes Server→Server. The pattern is:
- **Agent (on client)** edits a file in `~/GitHub/linebyline`
- **Syncthing** mirrors it to Server's `~/GitHub/linebyline` (via the Server's Docker-inotify-broken path)
- **Agent** invokes `~/.local/share/tst.sh` over `ssh Server`
- **`tst.sh` runs `podman run`** on the Server, which mounts `/home/user/GitHub/linebyline:/workspace` (host path) — which is the same bind-mount the Server's Syncthing writes into

So the 13.4s you measured from the client is the real number for the OMP loop. The 2s wait in the Phase 5 plan is too short. The 5s from fix A is also too short.

**Two things in conflict:**
1. The Syncthing delay is real and floor-bounded (5-15s in practice, not the 1-2s the inotify settings *should* give you).
2. The `tst.sh` loop doesn't need a deterministic wait — the file just needs to be there before `podman run` reads `/workspace`.

**Cleanest fix: `tst.sh` polls the Server's filesystem for the file before launching the container.** This makes the OMP loop self-synchronizing — no fixed wait, no race. The agent just edits and calls `tst.sh`; the script blocks until the server's view is current, then runs the tests.

The fish function (`tst.fish`) and the generated `tst.sh` both get the same change. Single source of truth, no drift:

```fish
function tst
  if not test -f playwright.config.js
    echo "tst: not in a Playwright repo (no playwright.config.js in $PWD)"
    echo "tst: cd into your repo first, e.g. cd ~/GitHub/linebyline"
    return 1
  end

  set -l remote Server
  set -l image mcr.microsoft.com/playwright:v1.61.0-noble
  set -l local_hash (git rev-parse HEAD 2>/dev/null; or git hash-object -- $(git ls-files | head -50) | sort | sha256sum)

  # Wait for Syncthing to converge: poll the server's view until its
  # working-tree mtime + size match the local state, up to 60s.
  ssh $remote "
    set -l local_hash '$local_hash'
    set -l attempt 0
    while [ \$attempt -lt 60 ]; do
      if [ -f /workspace/playwright.config.js ]; then
        set -l remote_hash (cd /workspace && git rev-parse HEAD 2>/dev/null)
        if [ \"\$remote_hash\" = \"\$local_hash\" ]; then
          echo SYNCED
          break
        fi
      fi
      set attempt (math \$attempt + 1)
      sleep 1
    end
  " | grep -q SYNCED || return 1

  ssh $remote "
    podman pull -q $image >/dev/null 2>&1
    podman run --rm -it \
      --userns=keep-id --security-opt label=disable \
      -v \$PWD:/workspace -w /workspace \
      -v ~/.local/share/podman-npm-cache:/tmp/.npm \
      -e HOME=/tmp -e NPM_CONFIG_CACHE=/tmp/.npm \
      -e NPM_CONFIG_UPDATE_NOTIFIER=false -e PW_CONTAINER=1 \
      $image npx playwright test \$argv
  "
end
```

This is overshooting — the simpler and more correct version uses mtime + a small list of files:

```fish
function tst
  if not test -f playwright.config.js
    echo "tst: not in a Playwright repo (no playwright.config.js in $PWD)"
    echo "tst: cd into your repo first, e.g. cd ~/GitHub/linebyline"
    return 1
  end

  set -l remote Server
  set -l image mcr.microsoft.com/playwright:v1.61.0-noble

  # Build a fingerprint of the local working tree (mtime + size, deterministic order).
  set -l local_state (find . -type f -not -path './.git/*' -not -path './node_modules/*' \
                       -printf '%T@ %s %p\n' | LC_ALL=C sort | sha256sum)

  ssh $remote "
    set -l local_state '$local_state'
    set -l attempt 0
    while [ \$attempt -lt 30 ]; do
      set -l remote_state (cd /workspace && find . -type f -not -path './.git/*' -not -path './node_modules/*' -printf '%T@ %s %p\n' | LC_ALL=C sort | sha256sum)
      if [ \"\$remote_state\" = \"\$local_state\" ]; then
        echo SYNCED
        exit 0
      fi
      set attempt (math \$attempt + 1)
      sleep 1
    end
    echo STALE >&2
    exit 1
  " || return 1

  ssh $remote "
    podman pull -q $image >/dev/null 2>&1
    podman run --rm -it \
      --userns=keep-id --security-opt label=disable \
      -v \$PWD:/workspace -w /workspace \
      -v ~/.local/share/podman-npm-cache:/tmp/.npm \
      -e HOME=/tmp -e NPM_CONFIG_CACHE=/tmp/.npm \
      -e NPM_CONFIG_UPDATE_NOTIFIER=false -e PW_CONTAINER=1 \
      $image npx playwright test \$argv
  "
end
```

**This is a Phase 4 change, not a Phase 3 change.** The plan's Phase 4 already includes the fish→bash generator, so adding the sync-wait is a 5-line addition to the body. Both the fish and the generated bash get the same code via the generator.

**What I recommend now:**
1. **Phase 3 stays as-is** — Syncthing is "Up to Date" in the GUI, the folder is shared, the `.stignore` is in place. The convergence verification marker (`.sync-test`) already passed.
2. **Skip the "2s wait" instruction in Phase 5.** Replace it with "use `tst.sh` from the project root; `tst.sh` synchronizes before running."
3. **Add the polling-sync body to Phase 4's `tst.fish`** when you rewrite it.
4. **Document the 5-15s Syncthing floor** in the plan's Setup reference as a known cost.

**About the Server→Server `ssh 192.168.1.254` "Permission denied":** that's the Server's `sshd_config` rejecting key auth for connections originating from itself (a `Match address 127.0.0.1` or `Match localuser` block, or `PubkeyAuthentication` defaulting off for some edge case). Doesn't matter for the OMP loop. The OMP agent only SSHes *from* the client *to* the Server, never Server→Server.

**About the 13.4s:** with the polling-sync body, `tst.sh` will block for exactly that long on the first run after an edit, then return immediately on subsequent runs (state already in sync). That's the right ergonomics.

The remote `ssh` is invoked as `ssh Server "..."` — and `Server` doesn't have a remote shell pinned in `~/.ssh/config`, so it uses the Server user's login shell, which is fish on Fedora 44. **The fix:** the remote shell needs to be fish, not bash. The `ssh $remote "..."` invocation needs to force fish on the Server, or the inner script needs to be rewritten in bash (or any POSIX shell).

Two ways to fix this:

**Option 1 — force fish on the Server's SSH session.** Add `bash -c 'exec fish -c "..."'` wrapping. Ugly because of nested escaping. Or use `ssh -t Server 'fish -c "..."'`. Or set the remote shell in the SSH config: `RemoteCommand fish` in the `Host Server` block, but that affects all SSH sessions and isn't desirable.

**Option 2 — rewrite the inner scripts in bash/POSIX.** The outer function stays in fish (because that's where the user runs `tst` interactively). The remote commands — both the sync-wait and the `podman run` — get rewritten as bash heredocs. The fish syntax `set -l x y` becomes bash syntax `x=y`, the `|| begin ... end` becomes `|| { ... ; }`, the `while` loop becomes a bash `while` loop. The outer function's `find`/fingerprint logic and the `ssh` invocation can stay in fish.

**Option 2 is correct.** The remote shell is a host property; your function should not depend on what the Server's default shell is. Portability is also a side benefit — if you ever run the Server from a non-Fedora box, it still works.

Three things this change does:

1. **`ssh $remote bash -s -- '...'`** — forces the remote shell to bash regardless of the Server's `/etc/passwd` entry. The `--` is the standard end-of-options marker; what follows is `$1` to bash.
2. **`<<'REMOTE'`** (with quoted delimiter) — sends the heredoc body verbatim. No shell variable expansion happens on the client. `$1`, `$attempt`, etc. are positional params *inside* the bash script, not fish vars.
3. **The `$local_state` and `$image` and `$argv`** in the `ssh ... -- '$local_state' '...'` lines are *fish* variables that get expanded by the client *before* ssh runs — that's what you want. They become positional params `$1`, `$2` to the remote bash.

**`or begin ... end` instead of `|| begin ... end`:** in fish, `cmd || begin ... end` is the error-handling construct. The plan's prior version had `|| begin ... end` which is actually a bash-style `||` that fish parses as "if cmd fails, run begin block as separate statement" — works in newer fish but `or begin ... end` is the canonical form and survives the sed generator better. The OMP agent's bash sees this as fish syntax anyway, so it doesn't matter for the OMP side.

**Now the real question:** did your previous edit apply the heredoc syntax I suggested, or did the file on disk never get the heredoc change? Let me re-read:

The error is `~/.config/fish/functions/tst.fish (line 26): Expected a string, but found a redirection` at the `<<'REMOTE'`. **Fish's parser is choking on the heredoc syntax** — fish *does* support heredocs in 3.4+, but its lexer disagrees with the SSH command's argument structure. Specifically, fish parses `ssh $remote bash -s -- '$local_state' <<'REMOTE'` as: `ssh` command, with arguments `$remote`, `bash`, `-s`, `--`, `'$local_state'`, and then sees `<<'REMOTE'` and complains because heredocs in fish are only allowed with certain commands and parsing contexts, and fish is misreading the quoted `'$local_state'` argument's quoting state.

**The simplest robust fix: stop using fish heredocs inside the function body. Use `printf %s` to send the script body to ssh's stdin, or write the script to a temp file.** That sidesteps fish's heredoc parser entirely.

Let me write the corrected `tst.fish` directly. The OMP sandbox can write to `/tmp` and the project dir, but not `~/.config` — so I'll write the corrected content to `/tmp/tst.fish.new` and tell you to `cp` it into place from a non-OMP shell:

ASSISTANT: Three changes from the broken version:

1. **No `<<'REMOTE'` heredocs.** Both remote scripts are stored in fish string variables (`set -l sync_script '...'`), then `printf %s` pipes them to `ssh`. Fish's parser doesn't get involved with shell redirection on the `ssh` line.

2. **The remote command is now `bash -s -- '$local_state'`, not `bash -s -- '$local_state' <<'REMOTE'`.** `bash -s --` reads the script from stdin (which is the printf output) and treats what follows `--` as positional params. This is the standard "run a bash script piped to ssh" idiom.

3. **Quoting inside the bash script body uses double-quotes** (e.g. `"./.git/*"`) instead of single-quotes (`'./.git/*'`). Because the script body is now a fish single-quoted string, single-quotes inside would be valid fish — but the bash script is going to be evaluated by bash, which needs its own quotes. Using double-quotes inside the bash body works because fish's `'...'` string passes them through literally.

The escaping of `"{print \$1}"` is a `printf`-in-bash-double-quote-awk-double-quote chain. The `\$1` is a backslash-escaped `$1` so bash doesn't expand it (it gets passed to awk as `$1` which is the field separator arg).

But the current fish file is the broken one because the `printf | ssh "bash -s -- '$x'"` pattern needs careful quoting that fish's single-quote-inside-double-quote-shell-arg doesn't handle. The fix is to *not* use shell-arg-passing for the data; use environment variables instead, which sidesteps every quoting issue:

- Set `local_state` and `image` as env vars on the client
- Export them through `ssh` (which forwards them as `LC_*` style env vars if marked `SendEnv`)
- Or simpler: use `bash -c '...'` where the script reads them from a different channel

**Cleanest fix: write the script to a temp file on the client, then `scp` it to the server, then `ssh server "bash /tmp/sync.sh $local_state"`**. That eliminates all shell-quoting games — the script is a file, the data is an arg, no heredocs, no printf piping, no nested quotes.

In fish 3.4+, the string syntax is: `\` only escapes `\` and `'`. So `\'` is a literal single quote. The string starting with `'` and containing `\'` would end at... the next non-escaped `'`. In fish: `'foo' 'bar'` is two adjacent string literals that get concatenated. So `'foo' \'bar'` is `'foo'` (string "foo") then `\'` (literal single quote char) then `'bar'` (string "bar") — the result is `foo'bar`. So in `'remote...\' \'` the structure is: string 1 ends at the first unescaped `'`, then `\'` is a literal `'`, then string 2 starts at the second `'`.

**This is exactly the bug.** Each `\'` in the line consumes one "string escape" but the string itself stays open across the line continuation, and the next `'` (on the same combined line) closes the string. **Two fixes needed:**

**Fix A — scope the `find` to the project root:** pass the absolute path to the function, or `cd` first. The current function uses `find .` which is whatever the user happens to be in. Add an explicit `cd (git rev-parse --show-toplevel)` (or use `git rev-parse --show-toplevel` as the find root):

if test -z "$project_root"

  -not -path "$project_root/.git/*" -not -path "$project_root/node_modules/*" \

**Fix B — fix the scp arg passing.** The `scp $sync_script $run_script $remote:tmp/` needs to be a single arg for the destination. In fish, `$remote:tmp/` should be a single token because there's no whitespace — but `:` is special in fish. Try quoting:

```

Or explicitly construct:
```fish
scp -q $sync_script $run_script "$remote":tmp/
```

`"$remote":tmp/` in fish concatenates the quoted string with `tmp/` as one token. This is the fish idiom for "the host called $remote, with the path tmp/". Same as the bash form.

Let me also fix the scp-silent-failure issue by checking exit status:

scp -q $sync_script $run_script "$remote":tmp/ || begin

But `git ls-files` doesn't capture mtime/size, so an edit to a tracked file won't change the fingerprint. The whole point of the fingerprint is to detect *edits*. So we need `git ls-files -m` (modified) + `git ls-files` (everything), or we need to use `git status` to detect dirty files and include their mtime/size in the hash.

The two repos' `git ls-files` outputs will match because both checkouts are in sync via Syncthing (the file *content* is mirrored), but the mtime of modified files will differ on the server vs client until Syncthing catches up.

Actually, **mtime is what we want** — the moment a file's content changes on the client, mtime updates. The server's copy of the same file will have a different (older) mtime until Syncthing finishes mirroring. The hash diverges → poll until they match → Syncthing has caught up.

But `git ls-files` is also valid if we hash `mtime` for the modified files. Or hash `(mtime, size, path)` only for files that differ from the committed state, plus a stable hash for clean files. That's complicated.

**On the fingerprint mismatch** (which I can't see directly from OMP either): the most likely causes given your `.stignore` patterns are files that the server has but the client doesn't, or vice versa. Looking at the `.stignore` from line 159-228 of the plan:

**The right fix is `git ls-files`**, not `find` with manual excludes. The server's `git ls-files` and the client's `git ls-files` produce the *same* list (modulo uncommitted changes) because both checkouts track the same set of committed files. And uncommitted modifications can be detected via `git status --porcelain` and added with their mtime.

**Even simpler approach** that avoids the fingerprint entirely: **use git's own index.** `git update-index --refresh` on the server checks whether the working tree matches the index. If it does, we're synced. If it doesn't, Syncthing hasn't finished. Run that in a loop:

```bash
# on the server
attempt=0
while [ $attempt -lt 60 ]; do
  if git -C /workspace update-index --refresh >/dev/null 2>&1; then
    echo SYNCED
    exit 0
  fi
  attempt=$((attempt + 1))
  sleep 1
done
echo STALE >&2
exit 1
```

`git update-index --refresh` re-stats every file in the index against the working tree and updates the index's stat info. If the working tree matches, it exits 0 silently. If files are stale or modified, it exits 1 with messages naming the offending files. This is exactly the "is the working tree current" check we want, and it's git's own idea of "current" — no need to enumerate or hash.

**This also solves the mtime-not-content problem cleanly:** if you've edited a tracked file, the server's copy is stale until Syncthing catches up; the server's `git update-index --refresh` will see the stale mtime and exit 1; we poll until it succeeds.

Let me rewrite with this approach:

```

**Why this approach is better than the fingerprint:**

1. **No `find` exclusions to maintain.** `git update-index --refresh` only looks at tracked files. Files ignored by `.gitignore` (and not committed) are invisible to git. Files that exist on the server but not the client (e.g. the server's `.stfolder/`) are also invisible. No enumeration, no exclusions.

2. **Captures mtime, not content.** When you edit a file, mtime changes locally, git's index says "stale", Syncthing mirrors the new file, server's mtime updates, git's index says "fresh". That's the loop.

3. **Reuses git's own state machine.** No need to hash anything; git does the comparison.

**Caveat:** `git update-index --refresh` also catches *uncommitted local modifications* on the server. If anything modifies files in the server's working tree outside of Syncthing (e.g. the test run writing to `playwright-report/`), the next `tst` call will see those as "stale" and wait. That's actually a feature: the OMP loop should wait for the working tree to stabilize before re-running tests, and a half-written report from the previous run is exactly the kind of instability we want to wait out. The previous fingerprint approach had the same issue (it excluded `playwright-report/`, but the test might write elsewhere).

If you find that `playwright-report` updates during a test run are blocking the next `tst`, the fix is to put the report dir on `.gitignore` and have git not track it (which it probably already does — let me check):

Same for `.omp/`, `.pi/`, `.stfolder/` if you have them gitignored (the second `ls-files` came back empty, so they're not tracked). The `git update-index --refresh` approach correctly excludes exactly the things that should be excluded, with no manual exclude list.

Both shells invoke `tst` from `$PATH`, the server's version does the podman run, the client just SSHes in:

```bash
#!/usr/bin/env bash
# tst — run Playwright tests on the Server
set -euo pipefail

remote=${TST_REMOTE:-Server}
image=${TST_IMAGE:-mcr.microsoft.com/playwright:v1.61.0-noble}
host_workspace=/home/user/GitHub/linebyline   # on the Server

# Sync-wait: poll the server's git index until it matches the working tree
attempt=0
while [ "$attempt" -lt 60 ]; do
  if ssh "$remote" "git -C $host_workspace update-index --refresh" >/dev/null 2>&1; then
    break
  fi
  attempt=$((attempt + 1))
  sleep 1
done
if [ "$attempt" -ge 60 ]; then
  echo "tst: working tree did not converge on the Server within 60s" >&2
  exit 1
fi

# Run the tests
ssh "$remote" "podman pull -q $image >/dev/null 2>&1
podman run --rm -it \
  --userns=keep-id --security-opt label=disable \
  -v $host_workspace:/workspace -w /workspace \
  -v \$HOME/.local/share/podman-npm-cache:/tmp/.npm \
  -e HOME=/tmp -e NPM_CONFIG_CACHE=/tmp/.npm \
  -e NPM_CONFIG_UPDATE_NOTIFIER=false -e PW_CONTAINER=1 \
  $image npx playwright test $*"
```

Three lines of pure bash. No fish function, no generator, no scp, no tmp files on the server, no quoting games. `$*` (unquoted) word-splits correctly for `npx playwright test $@` — that's idiomatic bash.

**The `set -euo pipefail` at the top means any failure aborts.** No silent failures.

**On running it from the server directly:** the same script (with the `ssh "$remote"` lines removed) is the on-server form. Two scripts, ~20 lines total, or one script that detects where it's running.

The hangs on the server are because of `podman run --rm -it` — the `-it` flags allocate a TTY. When the server's terminal isn't a TTY (which is your case — you ran the script via SSH or directly, no TTY allocation), `podman run` may hang waiting for TTY input or behave unpredictably. Or `npx playwright test --list` with no TTY hangs on a prompt. The `-it` flags are a leftover from interactive use.

Strip down the script to do *one thing* — run podman without `-it` — and use it the same way from both sides:

**Expected:** Playwright enumerates the tests. If it hangs, the issue is `podman run --rm` on the server. If it errors immediately, the error tells us what.

**Once that works from the server directly,** the client side becomes trivial — from your Ghostty fish prompt:

```fish
ssh Server /home/user/GitHub/linebyline/tests/tst --list
```

Three things to note about the script:

1. **`--rm` instead of `--rm -it`.** No TTY allocation. Works equally well from an interactive shell or a piped SSH session. The `-i` was the cause of the hang — podman tried to allocate a TTY and stalled.
2. **`podman pull ... || true`.** Doesn't abort if the image is already cached. `set -e` would otherwise kill the script on a non-zero pull exit.
3. **`exec podman run ...`.** Replaces the bash process with podman — no extra fork, no extra wait. Cleaner exit codes.

**No sync-wait in this version.** The `--list` should be near-instant after podman starts. For actual test runs, we add the sync-wait back later as a separate concern — but only after we know the core `podman run` works. Right now, "does the script run" is the question. Sync-wait is Phase 4.5, not Phase 4.

**The client-side `node_modules` proves the original local `tst` worked** (your Downloads copy). The original script *probably* ran `npm ci` inside the container before `npx playwright test`, or the host had `node_modules` available via a different mount.

**Two fixes:**

1. **Remove `/node_modules` from `.stignore` so Syncthing ships it to the server.** Tradeoff: ~50-200MB of node_modules mirrored in real time, conflicts on version bumps. Bad idea.
2. **Run `npm ci` (or `npm install`) inside the container before `npx playwright test`.** The image has node and npm; the project has a `package-lock.json`. This is the canonical approach.

**`npm ci` takes time** (~30-90s for a fresh install) and **also requires a cache for the npm modules** (the plan's `~/.local/share/podman-npm-cache:/tmp/.npm` mount is exactly for this). After the first run, subsequent runs use the cache and are fast.

Add `npm ci` to the script. This is also why the script's `-e HOME=/tmp -e NPM_CONFIG_CACHE=/tmp/.npm` is there — it tells npm to use the cache mount.

Wait — `npm ci` in a project without `node_modules` is what we want. But `package-lock.json` exists. The script needs to run `npm ci` before `npx playwright test`. Let me revise:

ASSISTANT: **Two changes:**

1. **`sh -c 'npm ci ... && npx playwright test "$@"' -- "$@"`** — runs `npm ci` inside the container before the test command. The `--` separates the script's args from `sh`'s, and `"$@"` at the end of `sh -c` is the positional args from the parent script (which are the test args).
2. **`--prefer-offline`** — npm uses the cache first, only fetches if missing. With a warm cache, `npm ci` is ~2s. Cold cache: ~30-90s for the first run.
3. **`--no-audit --no-fund`** — skips the network calls that hang or fail in containers.

**The `tests/tst` script is now self-contained: one container invocation that installs deps and runs tests.** No sync-wait, no client-side complexity. The `tests/tst` script is what `tst` is, from either side.

**But the 12s every run is what you're complaining about.** Let me check: `npm ci` deletes `node_modules` and reinstalls. Even with `--prefer-offline`, the **first run installs everything** to the cache, and **every subsequent `podman run` starts with an empty `/workspace/node_modules` because the container is ephemeral**. The host's `node_modules` exists (it's gitignored so Syncthing doesn't ship it to the server, but it's there on the client) — but the server's `node_modules` doesn't exist, and the container always has a fresh filesystem.

So every test run re-downloads 170 packages. **The `~/.local/share/podman-npm-cache:/tmp/.npm` mount caches the *download* but not the *extracted* `node_modules`.** Each run still does the full install (just faster, because the tarballs are cached).

**Your proposal is the right fix:** ship `node_modules` to the server via Syncthing. Then the container's `/workspace/node_modules` is already populated, `npm ci` is a no-op, and tests run immediately.

**Why I had `/node_modules` in `.stignore` originally:** Syncthing docs and common wisdom say to ignore `node_modules` because:
- It's huge (100-500MB)
- It's regenerable from `package-lock.json`
- Cross-platform issues (Windows, macOS, Linux have different binaries inside some packages)

**For your case (Linux-to-Linux, same Fedora, single user):** the standard concerns don't apply. Ship `node_modules`.

ssh Server tst --list
```

Expect the same 545 tests you saw before, ~5s on warm cache.

## 2. Now the SSH key implementation

**Plan from the parallel session, which I agree with:** create a dedicated, locked Playwright SSH key for the agent. The forced-command pattern via `command="..."` in `authorized_keys` makes the server reject anything except the test runner.

**Two ways to do this:**

### Option A — extend the existing `tst` script on the server (simpler, scoped to the test use case)

The current `tests/tst` is on the server (synced via Syncthing) and accepts Playwright args. The forced command can call it directly:

1. **Generate a dedicated key on the client:**
```sh
ssh-keygen -t ed25519 -N '' -C "agent-playwright-linebyline" -f ~/.ssh/id_ed25519_agent_playwright
```

2. **Prepend the forced command to the public key, then install on the server:**
```sh
# On the client
PUBKEY=$(cat ~/.ssh/id_ed25519_agent_playwright.pub)
# Wrap it with the forced command
printf 'command="/home/user/.local/bin/tst",no-port-forwarding,no-X11-forwarding,no-agent-forwarding,no-pty %s\n' "$PUBKEY" >> ssh-forced.pub

# Copy to server
scp ssh-forced.pub Server:
ssh Server 'mkdir -p ~/.ssh && cat ~/ssh-forced.pub >> ~/.ssh/authorized_keys && rm ~/ssh-forced.pub'
```

3. **Configure SSH on the client to use this key only for `Server`:**
```sh
# In ~/.ssh/config, add to the Server block:
#   IdentityFile ~/.ssh/id_ed25519_agent_playwright
#   IdentitiesOnly yes
```

4. **Verify the lock:**
```sh
# Should succeed (the lock runs tst --list, not what you typed):
ssh Server 'echo this never runs'
# Should fail (no shell, forced command only):
ssh Server 'ls /home/user'  # will instead run tst ls /home/user → likely fail with playwright error
```

The `command="..."` is interpreted by the server's `sshd`: any SSH connection using this key gets `command=...` as its `original command`, regardless of what the client typed. The server executes that command with the client's args appended. So `ssh Server 'ls /home/user'` becomes `tst ls /home/user` on the server, which fails because `npx playwright test ls /home/user` is invalid.

### Option B — wrap in a small custom shell (more flexible, but redundant)

A shell script on the server that calls `tst "$@"` and is the forced command. Same effect as Option A, just one more layer.

**I'd go with Option A.** It uses the existing `tst` script as the forced command, no extra wrapper. The `authorized_keys` line is the entire policy.

### Path A in detail

The forced command needs to handle a subtle case: the test runner needs to be in the `PATH` for the SSH session. When `sshd` runs the forced command, the user's shell startup (`~/.bashrc` etc.) is *not* sourced — the command is exec'd directly with `$PATH` set to the SSH-login default. On Fedora 44, the default `$PATH` for non-interactive SSH is `/usr/local/bin:/usr/bin`, no `~/.local/bin`. So `tst` won't be found.

**Fix:** call the script with its absolute path in the forced command:
```
command="/home/user/GitHub/linebyline/tests/tst",no-port-forwarding,no-X11-forwarding,no-agent-forwarding,no-pty ssh-ed25519 ...
```

This is the right form. No shell startup, no PATH dependency.

But wait — `tst` itself does `podman pull && podman run`. `podman` is at `/usr/bin/podman`, which is in the default PATH. `tests/tst` is at `/home/user/GitHub/linebyline/tests/tst`, an absolute path. Both work.

**Another concern:** `podman run` from the SSH-forced-command context runs as a *non-login* session. `set -euo pipefail` is at the top of `tst`, so any error aborts. The `podman pull` has `|| true` so a cached image doesn't fail. Should work.

**One more concern:** the OMP agent's `bwrap` sandbox hides `~/.ssh` behind `--tmpfs`. The locked key must be mounted *outside* the shadowed path. From the parallel session:

> Place the locked key in a separate path (outside the `--tmpfs` shadow):
> ```bash
> cp ~/.ssh/playwright-key ~/.config/omp/playwright-key
> chmod 600 ~/.config/omp/playwright-key
> ```
> Mount it specifically in your `bwrap` script:
> ```bash
> --ro-bind "$HOME/.config/omp/playwright-key" "/tmp/playwright-key"
> ```

For OMP, the path could be `~/.omp/playwright-key` (matching the existing `--bind $HOME/.omp $HOME/.omp` in your `safe-omp.sh`). That's already writable from inside the sandbox, so the agent can read it via the `~/.ssh` alias in the SSH config.

**Concretely:**

1. **On the client:** generate `~/.ssh/id_ed25519_agent_playwright` (or place it in `~/.omp/keys/agent_playwright` for the sandbox).

2. **SSH config snippet for `Server`:**
```
Host Server
  HostName 192.168.1.254
  User user
  IdentitiesOnly yes
  IdentityFile ~/.ssh/id_ed25519_agent_playwright
  # (your existing settings)
```

3. **On the server:** add to `~/.ssh/authorized_keys`:
```
command="/home/user/GitHub/linebyline/tests/tst",no-port-forwarding,no-X11-forwarding,no-agent-forwarding,no-pty ssh-ed25519 AAAA... agent-playwright-linebyline
```

4. **Test from your Ghostty prompt:**
```sh
ssh Server 'echo should not run'
# Expected: Playwright tries to run "echo should not run" as a test path → "no tests found" or similar error, NOT "should not run" in stdout
```

If the forced command works, you'll see Playwright output, not "should not run".

**This is what I'd actually do.** No `tests/tst` changes needed (it's already the right script). The only moving parts are the key, the SSH config, and the `authorized_keys` line.

### Sandbox path for the key

Your `safe-omp.sh` mounts `~/.ssh` as `--tmpfs` (so the agent can't see your master key). The locked key needs to be visible *somewhere* in the sandbox. Options:

- **`~/.omp/keys/agent_playwright`** + `--bind $HOME/.omp $HOME/.omp` (already exists in your script). The key is at `~/.omp/keys/agent_playwright` in the sandbox.
- **`/tmp/agent_playwright`** + a specific `--ro-bind` for it.

`~/.omp/keys/` is cleaner because it leverages the existing mount. Then in the SSH config:
```
IdentityFile ~/.omp/keys/agent_playwright
```
(relative to `$HOME`, which is the sandbox `$HOME`)

### Verification steps for the OMP agent (me, in the sandbox)

1. **Test that the original (master) key fails when invoked from OMP.** Run `ssh Server ls` from my OMP bwrap. Expected: `Permission denied (publickey)` because the master key is hidden behind `--tmpfs ~/.ssh`. **This proves the agent can't use your master key.** (If it can, the `--tmpfs` is wrong and the whole scheme is broken.)
2. **Test that the locked key works for `tst` calls.** Run `ssh Server -- 'echo should not run'` from OMP. Expected: Playwright error (the forced command runs `tst echo should not run`).
3. **Test the full `tst` invocation.** Run `ssh Server -- list` (or whatever) from OMP. Expected: Playwright enumerates tests.

If all three pass, the security model is working.

### One concern about step 3 — the "tool call times out while you wait for ~5 minutes"

The OMP bash tool has a 30-second default timeout. `ssh Server tst -g title` (the example from your log) took 19.6s, well under 30s. But a full suite (`ssh Server tst --list` enumerates 545 tests; the actual test run for all of them is probably 5+ minutes) will exceed 30s.

**Solutions:**
- `tst --list` (the smoke test): ~5-10s, under timeout. Safe.
- `tst -g "small pattern"` (filter to a few tests): typically 15-30s. Borderline.
- `tst` (full suite): 5+ minutes. **Will time out.**

**The OMP agent needs to be told:**
- Always pass `-g "small pattern"` or `tests/specific.spec.js` for runs that should complete within 30s.
- For full-suite runs, the agent should use a different mechanism (background the process, read the result file later, or extend the timeout). The OMP `--timeout 300` flag or the per-tool setting in `~/.omp/agent/config.yml` is the way.

This is a documented constraint of the integration, not a bug in `tst`.

**The OMP-side concern (step 5 in your list — "see whether the tool call times out"):** if `ssh Server -- -g title` takes >30s, my tool call fails and I report a timeout. That's a *known and expected* OMP behavior. The solution is to use longer-running invocations to run in the background and tail the output, or to scope tests via `-g` to keep runs short. ASSISTANT: **Important: yes, the sandbox will completely break SSH without an exception.** `--tmpfs $HOME/.ssh` means the sandbox sees an empty `$HOME/.ssh/` (no `config`, no `known_hosts`, no `agent` socket, no identities, no ControlMaster socket). `ssh Server` will:

- Fail to read `~/.ssh/config` → use defaults
- Have no `IdentityFile` → offer nothing to the server
- Have no `agent` socket → can't reach your cached KWallet-passphrases
- Have no `known_hosts` → would fail host-key verification (but the server is already in the host's `known_hosts`, which the sandbox can't see)
- Have no `sockets/` ControlMaster → can't multiplex

**So you need to add an exception.** Two ways:

### Option A — mount a curated `~/.ssh` directory with only what the agent needs

Create `~/.agent-ssh/` (or similar) on the host with:
- `config` (a stripped-down version referencing only the locked key, no `github.com` block, no master key reference)
- `known_hosts` (a copy with just the `Server` entry, not all of github.com etc.)
- The locked private key
- A unique `agent.sock` symlink if needed (or no agent)

Then in `safe-omp.sh`:
```bash
--bind "$HOME/.agent-ssh" "$HOME/.ssh"
```
or
```bash
--ro-bind "$HOME/.agent-ssh" "$HOME/.ssh"   # read-only so the agent can't modify its config
```

**Read-only is what you want.** The agent can't rewrite its `config` to point at the master key (which isn't there anyway, but defense in depth).

**But:** the locked key would then be inside the sandbox-visible `~/.ssh/`, and any plugin like `smart-approve` (or `omp-defender`) would likely have a "forbid writes to `~/.ssh/`" pattern that triggers a confirmation prompt. **That's annoying but safe.** And the key is read-only, so writes fail anyway.

**The path you suggested — `~/.omp/zzz/limited-entry/`** — bypasses this whole problem by keeping the key out of `~/.ssh/`. The SSH config in `~/.agent-ssh/config` references it via `IdentityFile /home/user/.omp/zzz/limited-entry/id_ed25519`. Same SSH connection works.

### Option B — keep the key outside `~/.ssh/` and use a custom `ssh -F` or `ssh -o IdentityFile=...`

The agent invokes SSH with explicit args:
```bash
ssh -F /home/user/.agent-ssh/config -i /home/user/.omp/zzz/limited-entry/id_ed25519 Server ...
```

This is fragile (the agent has to know the explicit paths) and defeats the purpose of `~/.ssh/config`. Not recommended.

### Option C — bind-mount just the key into a known path inside `~/.ssh`

Keep `~/.ssh` as `--tmpfs` (no config, no agent). Mount the locked key into a *known* path inside the empty `~/.ssh/`, and pass `-i` to ssh. Doesn't help — still no config, no agent, no known_hosts.

### My recommendation: Option A with the key OUTSIDE `~/.ssh`

This is the cleanest:

1. **Create `~/.agent-ssh/` on the host** with a minimal config:
   ```
   Host Server
       HostName 192.168.1.254
       User user
       IdentitiesOnly yes
       IdentityFile /home/user/.omp/zzz/limited-entry/id_ed25519_agent_playwright
       ServerAliveInterval 30
       ServerAliveCountMax 3
       UserKnownHostsFile /home/user/.agent-ssh/known_hosts
   ```
2. **Copy just the `Server` entry** to `~/.agent-ssh/known_hosts` (not the github entries).
3. **Place the locked private key at `/home/user/.omp/zzz/limited-entry/id_ed25519_agent_playwright`** (the obscure path you suggested).
4. **In `safe-omp.sh`:**
   ```bash
   --ro-bind "$HOME/.agent-ssh" "$HOME/.ssh"
   ```
   This replaces the `--tmpfs "$HOME/.ssh"` line. The sandbox now sees:
   - `~/.ssh/config` (read-only, only the Server block)
   - `~/.ssh/known_hosts` (read-only, only the Server entry)
   - No agent socket, no github key, no master key, no sockets/

5. **Server's `authorized_keys`:** install the locked public key with the forced command:
   ```
   command="/home/user/GitHub/linebyline/tests/tst",no-port-forwarding,no-X11-forwarding,no-agent-forwarding,no-pty ssh-ed25519 AAAA... agent-playwright-linebyline
   ```

**This gives the agent:**
- SSH access to `Server` only (via the locked key)
- Forced command = `tst` only (cannot run `rm`, `ls`, etc. on the server)
- No access to github (config has no `github.com` block, no `id_ed25519`)
- No access to master key (master key isn't in the sandbox at all)
- No write access to `~/.ssh/` (read-only bind)

**smart-approve considerations:** with the key at `~/.omp/zzz/limited-entry/`, the plugin's default "block writes to `~/.ssh/`, `.env`, etc." patterns don't fire on the key file (it's not in those paths). Reads are fine (RO-bind). The plugin would still prompt for the actual `ssh Server tst -g title` command if it considers `ssh` "risky", but that's a different layer and the user can pre-approve it for the session.

### On the `~/.omp/` vs `~/.agent-tools/` question

Your concern about `~/.omp/` collisions is valid — `omp-undo-redo` already lives outside `plugins/`. If a future OMP feature happens to scan `~/.omp/` recursively, it might trip on the key directory. `~/.agent-tools/limited-entry/` is a more honest namespace (it's not part of OMP at all). I prefer your `~/.agent-tools/` suggestion:

```
/home/user/.agent-tools/limited-entry/id_ed25519_agent_playwright
/home/user/.agent-tools/limited-entry/id_ed25519_agent_playwright.pub
```

**And the SSH config's IdentityFile path becomes:**
```
IdentityFile /home/user/.agent-tools/limited-entry/id_ed25519_agent_playwright
```

ASSISTANT: **On #2 — co-locating config and key: it's actually a sensible pattern, and there's a real name for it.** A folder containing `config`, private key, public key, and `known_hosts` is a complete SSH client profile. Using it via `ssh -F <path>/config ...` or by symlinking it to `~/.ssh/` is the standard "portable SSH identity" approach (git-crypt, secret-management tools, and dotfile repos like `yadm` do this). The `--ro-bind` of one folder gives you all four files atomically — no risk of a half-updated state where the config references a key the bind hasn't shipped yet. The only reason it's not the *default* is historical: OpenSSH's `~/.ssh/` location was set in 1995 and changing it would break billions of configs.

**One thing to double-check before you commit to this:** the `known_hosts` entry has to match the SSH client. If `ssh -F /home/user/.agent-tools/limited-entry/config Server` works with the config, the same config's `UserKnownHostsFile` setting (or the default `~/.ssh/known_hosts` if not set) determines where host keys are looked up. We'll either copy the `Server` line from the host's `known_hosts` into `~/.agent-tools/limited-entry/known_hosts`, or set `UserKnownHostsFile` explicitly. I'll do the explicit setting.

1. **Run the setup script on the host** (your Ghostty fish prompt):
   ```fish
   bash /tmp/setup-agent-ssh.sh
   ```
   This generates the key, creates the profile folder, populates `config` and `known_hosts`, and prints the `authorized_keys` line.

2. **Install the public key on the Server.** The script prints the exact line. Use `ssh Server 'tee -a ~/.ssh/authorized_keys >/dev/null'` and paste, or use a heredoc:
   ```fish
   ssh Server 'cat >> ~/.ssh/authorized_keys' < ~/.agent-tools/limited-entry/id_ed25519_agent_playwright.pub
   ```
   Then prepend the `command="..."` options manually. Or do it the safe way:
   ```fish
   # Build the full authorized_keys line on the host, then append on server
   set line "command=\"/home/user/.local/bin/tst\",no-port-forwarding,no-X11-forwarding,no-agent-forwarding,no-pty "(cat ~/.agent-tools/limited-entry/id_ed25519_agent_playwright.pub)
   printf '%s\n' "$line" | ssh Server 'cat >> ~/.ssh/authorized_keys'
   ```
   (Fish syntax; the double-quotes inside the single-quoted fish string pass through.)

3. **Edit `~/.bash/safe-omp.sh`** from your Ghostty prompt: replace the `--tmpfs "$HOME/.ssh"` line with the `--ro-bind` line above.

4. **Verify from your normal shell (no OMP yet):**
   ```fish
   ssh Server 'echo should not run'
   ```
   Expected: Playwright tries to run `tst echo should not run` → fails with "no tests found" or similar; you do NOT see `should not run` echoed. If you see `should not run`, the forced command isn't working.

5. **Verify the right way works:**
   ```fish
   ssh Server -- list
   ```
   (The `--` is ssh's end-of-options; `list` is passed to `tst` as args.) Expected: `npx playwright test list` runs and shows the test list. Should be ~5s.

6. **Then I do the OMP-sandbox verification:**
   - Test that arbitrary `ssh Server ls` fails (master key hidden, only locked key visible)
   - Test that `ssh Server -- -g title` works (forced command runs tst, which runs Playwright)
   - Test the timeout constraint: a `--list` should fit in 30s, but a full suite won't

7. **Update `safe-omp.sh` validation in the plan:** the file change is small enough not to warrant a plan update; add a one-liner to Phase 2's bwrap section.

## On the `tst` script path question

You mentioned: "I moved the script to bin... left the original copy in tests for you to work with now but will clean that up after this session."

**The forced command in `authorized_keys` is an absolute path baked into the server's `authorized_keys`. If you later delete `~/.local/bin/tst` on the server, the forced command breaks — SSH connections using this key fail with "no such file or directory".** That's actually *good*: it's a hard failure mode, not a silent bypass. But it does mean you need to keep `~/.local/bin/tst` on the server for as long as you use this key.

**Two options for cleanup later:**
- (a) Leave `~/.local/bin/tst` on the server, delete `tests/tst` (the Syncthing-tracked copy is now redundant for the server — the server's `tst` is in `~/.local/bin/`, not in the project tree).
- (b) Update the forced command in `authorized_keys` to point at the Syncthing path: `command="/home/user/GitHub/linebyline/tests/tst",...`. Then deleting `~/.local/bin/tst` on the server is fine.

(a) is what you're doing already. Just make sure the deletion order is: (1) keep `~/.local/bin/tst` forever, (2) delete `tests/tst` whenever you want. **Don't delete `~/.local/bin/tst`** — it's the policy anchor.

The `tests/tst` file I just edited is the one I was working with per your note. It's the same script either way (the bash one-liner doesn't care where it lives). The file I wrote is at `~/GitHub/linebyline/tests/tst` and will get Syncthing-shipped to the server's same path. **The forced command's path is independent** — it can point at either `~/.local/bin/tst` or `tests/tst` (both exist on the server after Syncthing sync).

If you want a single source of truth on the server, point the forced command at `tests/tst` (Syncthing-managed, version-controlled, easy to update). Then `~/.local/bin/tst` becomes optional (just a convenience for direct keyboard use). I'd lean that way. But the choice is yours.

ASSISTANT: **Two issues, both visible:**

## Issue 1: The forced command is on a separate line from the key

Look at the `authorized_keys` on the server:
```
command="/home/user/.local/bin/tst",no-port-forwarding,no-X11-forwarding,no-agent-forwarding,no-pty
ssh-ed25519 AAAA... agent-playwright-linebyline
```

**The options line and the key line are on separate lines.** `authorized_keys` parses each line as one entry; the first line is just options with no key (invalid, ignored), the second line is a key with no options (no force, just plain key). So the agent's key works as a normal key with no forced command — `ssh Server 'echo should not run'` ran `echo` and printed it, and `ssh Server -- list` ran `list` as a shell command and printed "command not found".

**Fix:** put the entire entry on one line. The `kwrite` save put a hard newline between the options and the key. Need to merge them.

## Issue 2: The `known_hosts` doesn't matter for the SSH-key-trust path

Actually wait — `ssh-keyscan -H` added entries to `known_hosts`, but the first test `ssh Server 'echo should not run'` *worked* (printed "should not run"). So host key verification passed. **The `known_hosts` issue from the script was a false alarm** — your system `known_hosts` was using the old non-hashed format, and my grep didn't match. The host key was there, ssh verified it, the connection succeeded.

So Issue 2 is non-blocking. The only real bug is Issue 1.

## Fix Issue 1 from the server keyboard

You're on the server, so you can re-edit `~/.ssh/authorized_keys` directly. The current content is:
```
ssh-ed25519 <oldkey> PC→Server
command="/home/user/.local/bin/tst",no-port-forwarding,no-X11-forwarding,no-agent-forwarding,no-pty
ssh-ed25519 AAAA... agent-playwright-linebyline
```

**It should be:**
```
ssh-ed25519 <oldkey> PC→Server
command="/home/user/.local/bin/tst",no-port-forwarding,no-X11-forwarding,no-agent-forwarding,no-pty ssh-ed25519 AAAA... agent-playwright-linebyline
```

One line, the options and key joined. **Easiest fix from the server console without a text editor:**

```sh
# 1. Remove the orphaned options line
sed -i '/^command="\/home\/user\/.local\/bin\/tst"/d' ~/.ssh/authorized_keys

# 2. Re-append the entry as a single line
# (paste the whole line from the client; the public key is there)
```

Or, do it from the client without needing `vim` on the server. **Use the host's clipboard or a here-doc:**

From the client, the public key is at `~/.agent-tools/limited-entry/id_ed25519_agent_playwright.pub`. The forced command prefix is constant. So:

```fish
# On the client
set line "command=\"/home/user/.local/bin/tst\",no-port-forwarding,no-X11-forwarding,no-agent-forwarding,no-pty "(cat ~/.agent-tools/limited-entry/id_ed25519_agent_playwright.pub)

# 1. Remove the broken entry (the standalone options line)
ssh Server "sed -i '/^command=\"\\/home\\/user\\/.local\\/bin\\/tst\"/d' ~/.ssh/authorized_keys"

# 2. Append the corrected single-line entry
printf '%s\n' "$line" | ssh Server "cat >> ~/.ssh/authorized_keys"
```

Or even simpler, from the client in one command:

```fish
ssh Server "sed -i '/^command=\"\\/home\\/user\\/.local\\/bin\\/tst\"/d' ~/.ssh/authorized_keys && echo 'command=\"/home/user/.local/bin/tst\",no-port-forwarding,no-X11-forwarding,no-agent-forwarding,no-pty '\"\$(cat ~/.agent-tools/limited-entry/id_ed25519_agent_playwright.pub)\"' >> ~/.ssh/authorized_keys"
```

ASSISTANT: **The dry run on the client produces the correct full entry (208 chars).** The `entry` variable is right. So the bug is in the SSH transmission, not the build.

**The `ssh Server bash -s -- "$entry"` is the suspect.** When bash receives a `--` followed by a positional arg, the arg becomes `$1`. But the arg is passed *over the wire via SSH*, which means the shell on the *host* serializes it into the SSH command line. The command line becomes:

```
bash -s -- command="/home/user/.local/bin/tst",no-port-forwarding,no-X11-forwarding,no-agent-forwarding,no-pty ssh-ed25519 AAAA... agent-playwright-linebyline
```

The shell on the *server* parses this. It sees `bash` invoked with `-s --` and then several words. **But wait — the `"` quotes in the command-line are interpreted by the host's shell when constructing the SSH command, not by the remote's shell.** The remote's `bash -s` only sees the de-quoted values.

Actually that's not how SSH works. SSH passes the args to the remote shell as a single string, which the remote shell parses. So if the host's shell passes:
```
bash -s -- 'command="/home/user/.local/bin/tst",...' 'ssh-ed25519' 'AAAAC...' 'agent-playwright-linebyline'
```
…then the remote's `bash -s` would see `$0=bash`, `$1=command="/home/user/.local/bin/tst",...`, `$2=ssh-ed25519`, `$3=AAAAC...`, `$4=agent-playwright-linebyline`. The single quotes in the host's quoting protect each space-separated word.

But the host's `ssh ... "$entry"` is **double-quoting the whole entry** as one arg. So the SSH command line is:
```
bash -s -- command="/home/user/.local/bin/tst",no-port-forwarding,no-X11-forwarding,no-agent-forwarding,no-pty ssh-ed25519 AAAA... agent-playwright-linebyline
```

That's *one* word to SSH, but SSH splits it on spaces before passing to the remote. The remote's `bash -s --` then sees it as **multiple args**, not one.

**This is the bug.** `ssh Server bash -s -- "$entry"` passes `entry` to the remote as a string, but the remote's shell re-parses it as multiple args. The `entry` has 4 words (the options string is one, the key type is another, the key body is the third, the comment is the fourth), so `$1` on the remote only gets the first word — the options prefix.

**The fix:** don't pass the entry as an arg; pass it via stdin (the heredoc body). Or base64-encode and decode on the remote.

Cleanest: **write the entry to a temp file on the host, scp it to the server, and have the remote script read it.**

ASSISTANT: **Three changes from the previous version:**

1. **Writes `entry` to a temp file, scp's to server.** No SSH arg-passing of the entry value — the server reads it from `/tmp/agent-key-entry` instead of `$1`.
2. **`ssh Server bash -s` (no `-- "$entry"`).** No positional arg passed via SSH command line.
3. **`set -e` at top of remote heredoc** — explicit error handling on the remote side (it might be missing in the server's default bash interactive config).

**Then test the lock from the client:**

```fish
ssh Server 'echo should not run'
```

**Important:** the existing SSH connection has the master key cached in `known_hosts` and likely the SSH agent. After the cleanup, the master key still works (it has no `command=`, so the agent's request runs normally). So `ssh Server 'echo should not run'` will still print `should not run` because **the master key has no forced command**.

**To test the locked key:** you need to force the client to use the locked key, not the master key. The way to do that:
```fish
ssh -i ~/.agent-tools/limited-entry/id_ed25519_agent_playwright -o IdentitiesOnly=yes Server 'echo should not run'
```

That bypasses your `~/.ssh/config` (no `Server` block) and uses the locked key directly. With `IdentitiesOnly=yes`, ssh only offers that key. The server's `authorized_keys` matches it, applies the forced command `tst echo should not run`, and Playwright errors out instead of printing "should not run".

**This is also what the OMP agent will need to do** — except the agent's `~/.ssh/config` is the curated one in `~/.agent-tools/limited-entry/config`, which already has `IdentitiesOnly yes` and `IdentityFile ~/.agent-tools/limited-entry/id_ed25519_agent_playwright`. So inside the OMP sandbox, `ssh Server ...` will use only the locked key.

**Two ways to fix, with very different security implications:**

**Option 1 — allow args pass-through (what you originally intended).** Make the forced command forward the original command to `tst`. This requires the `command=` to be:
```

```

Actually the correct form is:
```
command="/home/user/.local/bin/tst $SSH_ORIGINAL_COMMAND",...
```
But `command=` values in authorized_keys don't do shell expansion of `$SSH_ORIGINAL_COMMAND` reliably across versions, and it breaks the security model (injection via the command string).

**Option 2 — keep args dropped (maximally locked), and accept the agent can only run the full suite.** The agent runs `agent-tst` (no args) → full suite. Useless for scoped iteration, but bulletproof security.

**Option 3 — a curry/semantic middle ground:** the forced command runs a *wrapper* that parses only whitelisted args and rejects everything else. E.g.:
```
command="/home/user/.local/bin/tst-whitelist",...
```
where `tst-whitelist` only allows specific flags (`-g PATTERN`, `--list`, `tests/FOO.spec.js` matching `tests/`) and passes them through after validation. This is the best balance.
