## What shipped

Three working scripts in `~/GitHub/linebyline/tests/` (synced to the Server via Syncthing, with Server-side symlinks to `~/.local/bin/`):

- **`tests/tst`** — pure-Bash one-shot `podman run` Playwright runner. `$@` → `npx playwright test "$@"`. No `npm ci` (node_modules ships via Syncthing). `TST_HOST_WS`/`TST_IMAGE` configurable.
- **`tests/tst-locked`** — Server-side `command=` target. Reads `$SSH_ORIGINAL_COMMAND`, allowlists `-g/--grep` (strict charset), `--list`, `tests/*.spec.js`, `--project=`; rejects everything else; then execs `tst`.
- **`tests/agent-tst`** — the OMP agent's only entry point. `ssh -F <profile>/config -o BatchMode=yes Server -- "$@"`.

Plus docs: **`tests/SSH_SETUP.md`** (reusable two-key + `command=` + whitelist pattern) and **`tests/PLAYWRIGHT_SETUP.md`** (Headless section extended, backlinks SSH_SETUP). The drifted plan doc got an authoritative "FINAL STATE" section.

## Security model proven

- ✅ **Scoped tests via restricted key:** `agent-tst -g title` → **3 passed (14.9s)**, exit 0. Also `--list` works.
- ✅ **Arbitrary commands blocked:** `agent-tst 'rm -rf /'` → `tst-locked: rejected: rm`, exit 1.
- ✅ **Injection blocked:** `-g "title; whoami"` → `tst-locked: invalid -g pattern`, exit 1.
- ✅ **Master key unreachable from sandbox:** sandbox `~/.ssh/` contains only the restricted profile — no `id_ed25519` or `id_ed25519_server`.

## The three hard-won gotchas (in `SSH_SETUP.md` and the plan's final state)

1. **`$SSH_ORIGINAL_COMMAND` is the only reliable arg source** for a `command=` key (appended `$@` gave `args(0)` here).
2. **`ssh -g title` lets ssh eat `-g` as its own GatewayPorts flag** — the wrapper needs `--` to forward `-g` verbatim.
3. **`authorized_keys` line must be one physical line** — a newline silently drops the restriction.

## One decision left to you

`~/Downloads/tst.fish` (the original local-only runner) is fully superseded and unreferenced, but it's your file in `~/Downloads`. I left it rather than delete it. Delete it with `rm ~/Downloads/tst.fish` when you're ready; the host-level `/tmp/*.sh` helpers auto-clean on reboot.

**Residual security note for future sessions:** the agent's write access to `~/.agent-tools/` (via the `~/.omp`-style bind) isn't in play — the restricted key needs no client-side key write once set up, and `tests/tst`/`tests/tst-locked` on the server are only editable from the client master-key side. If you later run `smart-approve`, the `-F`-based wrapper is the single command to pre-approve.

USER: Is it feasible to consolidate the three different scripts (`tst`,`tst-locked`,`agent-tst`) cleanly back into the original `tst` script? If not, is it primarily a logical (i.e. concerns have to be split for the workaround to function), architectural (i.e. would a Python script have an easier time?), or environmental (i.e. system configuration outside the script) barrier?

ASSISTANT: **Feasible, but it would be a downgrade.** Here's the honest breakdown — the split is *architectural by necessity*, not a scripting-language or environmental accident.

## Why they can't merge into one `tst`

The three scripts run on **different hosts with different trust domains**:

| Script | Runs on | Trust domain |
|--------|---------|--------------|
| `tst` | Server | Trusted shell (both human and `command=`-wrapped) |
| `tst-locked` | Server | The enforcement point — **untrusted input** (`$SSH_ORIGINAL_COMMAND`) |
| `agent-tst` | Client | The **untrusted agent's** only surface |

The boundary isn't arbitrary — it's the sshd `authorized_keys` line `command="/home/user/.local/bin/tst-locked"`. That line **names exactly one file**. It cannot point to "a function inside another script." The forced command is a path, so the whitelist validator *must* be a standalone executable file on the server. That's the structural reason `tst-locked` can't fold into `tst`: `tst` is the *untrusted-input sink*, and a validator sitting inside the thing it's supposed to validate is a textbook-confused-deputy setup. You'd be validating `$SSH_ORIGINAL_COMMAND` with a script the same input can (in principle) influence.

`agent-tst` is on the **client**, a completely different machine — it can't be merged with server-side files for the trivial reason that it runs on a different host.

## Could a single script detect its role?

Yes, but it would make the security *worse*, not *better*. I actually wrote exactly that in the very first pure-Bash draft (the `hostname = fedora` branch) before abandoning it. A single script that does:

```bash
if server: validate args, run tst
if client: ssh Server -- ...
```

recreates the confused-deputy problem: the *same bytes* both validate input and execute it. An attacker who can influence one path now influences the validator's logic too. And it irretrievably blurs "what does the restricted key actually run?" — the audit question (`at /path, what does this key run?`) becomes "depends on hostname, which I can't see from outside."

## The three files aren't really "three separate concerns" though

Look closer: `tst-locked` and `agent-tst` are both **thin wrappers** (~40 lines each) whose entire job is to point at the right ssh config / enforce the allowlist. The *real* logic (`tst`) is one file. If you want less sprawl, the clean consolidation is **two files, not one**:

- `tst` (the runner, server-side) — unchanged
- one wrapper that does both directions

But even that fails on the client/server split: the client wrapper runs `ssh -F <profile>`, the server wrapper reads `$SSH_ORIGINAL_COMMAND`. They share no code. Merging them would be a single script with a `hostname`/`$SSH_ORIGINAL_COMMAND`-present branch — the confused-deputy form again.

## Lingua franca: would Python help?

**No.** Anything that runs the same way on both hosts and reads the same env var has the identical split. Python doesn't change the trust-domain boundary. In fact it'd *hurt*:

- Python adds a language-runtime dependency on the Server (you'd have to verify a specific Python is present, versus the `/bin/bash` that's guaranteed).
- The xkcd-line risk: `$SSH_ORIGINAL_COMMAND` into a parser is the same injection surface.
- Bash-with-`grep -Eq` allowlist is actually *cleaner* here than Python string parsing — the strict charset regex is a shell-native one-liner.

## The one legitimate consolidation

What you *can* cleanly merge: `tst` + a defensively-suffixed runner, since on the server `tst-locked` and `tst` both execute under the same trust domain (both are the *authority*, running on the server as `user`). You could collapse `tst-locked`'s validation *into* `tst` itself, and have `command=` point at `tst`. That's 2 files instead of 3:

```
command="/home/user/.local/bin/tst" → tst does: validate $SSH_ORIGINAL_COMMAND (if present), then podman run
```

**But** this is exactly the case I'd push back on: it means the human's `ssh Server tst -g title` (master key, no `$SSH_ORIGINAL_COMMAND`) skips validation, while the agent's call through the locked key (which *sets* `$SSH_ORIGINAL_COMMAND`) validates. So validation becomes conditional on an env var being set — which is *precisely* what a determined attacker (or a prompt injection that gets the agent to set/clear env) nibbles at. The separate `tst-locked` makes the invariant "the restricted key ALWAYS goes through the validator" structurally guaranteed by the file path, not by a runtime `if`.

## Verdict

| Barrier | Contribution |
|---------|--------------|
| **Logical** | High — the validator *must* not be the thing it validates (confused deputy) |
| **Architectural** | High — one file is named by `authorized_keys`; two others run on a different host |
| **Environmental** | Low — no system config forces the split; `/bin/bash` is everywhere, Python adds nothing |

It's **primarily an architectural/trust-domain split** — the three files exist because the trust boundaries (client vs. server) and the enforcement point (`command=` names one path) are real, not syntactic. The cost of the third file is ~40 lines; the cost of collapsing it is a weaker security invariant. Keep the split — the `tst-locked` file *is* the security mechanism, not an implementation detail.

If you want to reduce the file count for aesthetics without weakening it, the honest options are: (1) keep all three (current), or (2) treat `tst-locked` as the canonical server entry and fold the *agent-tst* client wrapper into your agent's `$PATH` alias/abbr so the client side reads as a single `tst` command to the human (the file exists but the human never sees it). Option 2 is what I'd reach for if the 3-file layout bothers you — it hides the client wrapper without touching the security boundary.

ASSISTANT: Your table is close, but I need to correct it before editing SSH_SETUP.md — the client `~/.local/bin` vs `$PATH` question is the subtle part.

## The two real errors in your table

**Error 1: `tst` should exist on the client as `~/.local/bin/tst`, not "Fish alias for user only".**

Both hosts have `/home/user/.local/bin` on `$PATH` (standard Fedora `~/.bash_profile` / fish's default path includes `~/.local/bin`). You already have `~/.local/bin/tst` **symlinked to `tests/tst` on both hosts** (that's how the server's forced command resolves, and how `ssh Server tst` reached it). So on the client, `tst` isn't a Fish alias — it's a real executable on `$PATH`. A Fish alias is fragile (only fish sees it; bash/OMP never would). Symlinking `~/.local/bin/tst → tests/tst` works in **every** shell. That's the non-atomic part of the original dream that's worth keeping: `tst` on the client is the human+agent's uniform entry point, it's shell-agnostic, and it's the same file synced to the server.

**Error 2: `tst-lock` should actually be `tst-locked`, and the filename matters because `authorized_keys` names it.**

Your `authorized_keys` line is baked to `/home/user/.local/bin/tst-locked`. If you rename the file to `tst-lock`, the forced command breaks. Keep the exact filename `tst-locked` unless you also re-edit `authorized_keys`.

## Corrected matrix

| File | Server | Client | Role |
|------|--------|--------|------|
| `tst` | `~/.local/bin/tst` → `tests/tst` (symlink) | `~/.local/bin/tst` → `tests/tst` (symlink) | The podman runner. Present & identical on both hosts. |
| `tst-locked` | `~/.local/bin/tst-locked` → `tests/tst-locked` (symlink) — **named by `authorized_keys`** | **not needed** (never called locally) | The `command=` enforcement wrapper (server-only in practice). |
| `agent-tst` | not needed | `~/.local/bin/agent-tst` → `tests/agent-tst` | The client-side restricted-key wrapper (obsolesces the `tst` symlink for agent-local runs). |

## The Option 2 consolidation

Now — the point of your Option 2 is to hide the client wrapper so the *human* registers a single `tst` command, while the *agent* uses the distinct `agent-tst`. Two place to put the wrapper:

**Path A — rename `agent-tst` → `tst` on the client, keep server `tst`.** This is the "client and server both have a `tst` but they're different files" approach, which is confusing — two different `tests/tst` vs `tests/agent-tst` files. Rejected.

**Path B (my recommendation) — keep three files, but make `agent-tst` a `$PATH` alias that shadows `tst` only for shell-local use, OR fold `agent-tst`'s content into `tst`'s client behavior.** 

Actually, let me think about what "hide the wrapper" really means. The cleanest Option 2:

**On the client, `~/.local/bin/tst` should be `agent-tst`'s content (the ssh -F restricted path), NOT the server `tst`.** Because the client should *never* `podman run` locally — it always SSHes to the server. So the client's "tst" command is inherently the ssh wrapper, and the server's "tst" command is inherently the podman runner. They're different files doing different things on different hosts — which is *fine* and *clearer* than pretending they're the same.

So the real Option 2 layout:

| File | Server `~/.local/bin/` | Client `~/.local/bin/` |
|------|------------------------|------------------------|
| `tst` | → `tests/tst` (podman) | → `tests/agent-tst` (ssh restricted) — **the client wrapper IS named `tst`** |

- Server: `~/.local/bin/tst` → `tests/tst` (podman runner)

| File | Client `~/.local/bin/` | Server `~/.local/bin/` | Role |

The whole point of Option 2 is to give the agent a working command. So the client needs a real `~/.local/bin/agent-tst` **symlink** (shell-agnostic), not just the human's fish abbr.

2. **`tst-lock` ≠ `tst-locked`.** `authorized_keys` is baked to `/home/user/.local/bin/tst-locked`. Rename = break.

## The clean consolidation (what I'd actually recommend)

Your Option 2 impulse — "hide the client wrapper" — resolves to this: **the agent gets a `~/.local/bin/agent-tst` symlink → `tests/agent-tst`.** It's a real executable on `$PATH`, so bash/fish/OMP all find it. The human keeps their fish `abbr tst 'ssh Server tst'` for the master-key path, OR I can point their `tst` at the restricted wrapper too. The file `agent-tst` exists (versioned), but to the *user* the mental model stays "type `tst`".

**But your table needs one more correction about who runs what.** The `agent-tst` symlink on the client PATH + the OMP agent's sandbox `~/.ssh` (restricted profile) = the agent runs `agent-tst -g title` and it works through the locked key. That's what makes the whole thing function — the agent must use the restricted `-F` path, NOT the master-key abbr.

So: yes, your table is essentially right with these fixes:
- `tst` server: `~/.local/bin/tst` → `tests/tst` ✅
- `tst` client: it's a fish `abbr`, and the **agent** needs `~/.local/bin/agent-tst` → `tests/agent-tst` (a real symlink, not an alias)
- `tst-locked` server: `~/.local/bin/tst-locked` → `tests/tst-locked`, filename exact ✅ (your table wrote `tst-lock`, fix that)
- `agent-tst` client: `~/.local/bin/agent-tst` → `tests/agent-tst` ✅ (this is the symlink that makes the agent work)

Reason: the human's fish `abbr tst 'ssh Server tst'` uses the master key (interactive, full shell — you WANT that for debugging). If `tst` on the client pointed at `agent-tst` (restricted key), you'd lose master-key access to the server from the `tst` command, and `ssh Server tst` would become `ssh Server agent-tst`... no wait, that breaks. Keep them distinct:

- `tst` (client, human abbr) → master key, for interactive debugging
- `agent-tst` (client, symlink) → restricted key, for the agent

That clean separation is the whole point of two keys. Don't collapse them.

ASSISTANT: **That's cleaner and I agree with most of it, but there's one hard constraint that changes your plan, and one subtlety about which key `agent-tst` actually uses.**

## The hard constraint: `tst`'s own path is a self-reference

Look at `tst-locked`'s last line:

```bash
exec /home/user/.local/bin/tst "${clean[@]:-}"
```

It calls `tst` by **absolute path** `/home/user/.local/bin/tst`. So on the **server**, `tst` MUST live at `~/.local/bin/tst` — which is where your plan puts it. ✅ Good, that's consistent.

But here's the problem: **the scripts currently reference each other through the synced `tests/` path in places, and your "no symlinks, none in tests/" plan breaks the iteration loop you're currently using.**

## Where your plan conflicts with reality

**Conflict 1: `tst-locked` execs `/home/user/.local/bin/tst`, and you want `tst` moved out of `tests/` into `~/.local/bin/` directly.** That's *fine* on the server — `tst-locked` execs the absolute `~/.local/bin/tst`. No symlink needed. Good.

**Conflict 2: You want to remove `tst`, `tst-locked` from `tests/` on BOTH sides.** But `tests/tst`, `tests/tst-locked`, `tests/agent-tst` are currently synced via Syncthing and are how you iterate quickly. If you remove them from `tests/`:
- The server no longer gets updates to `tst`/`tst-locked` from the client repo
- You'd have to edit them directly on the server (which is how you wanted it — only the human edits server scripts)

That's actually the security win you're describing: **only the human (master key) edits `~/.local/bin/tst` on the server; no synced copy means no agent can modify them via the repo.** Correct and desirable.

**But there's a wrinkle: `agent-tst` on the client.** You want `~/.local/bin/agent-tst` on the client. That's fine. But `agent-tst` currently references the profile config via:
```bash
profile_dir=${TST_AGENT_PROFILE:-$HOME/.agent-tools/limited-entry}
exec ssh -F "$profile_dir/config" ...
```
That's `$HOME`-relative, environment-independent. Works from `~/.local/bin/agent-tst` regardless. ✅

## The one thing that must NOT change: `authorized_keys` path

Your server `authorized_keys` has `command="/home/user/.local/bin/tst-locked"`. As long as `tst-locked` stays at `~/.local/bin/tst-locked`, that's fine. Your plan keeps it there. ✅

## The subtlety about `agent-tst` and the master key

You said *"I think `agent-tst` is already covered by `--ro-bind "$HOME" "$HOME"` on the client."* — but `agent-tst` doesn't need a bind for itself; it's read-only code. What matters is **`~/.agent-tools/limited-entry/`** (the restricted key profile) is reachable. It IS, via `--ro-bind "$HOME/.agent-tools/limited-entry" "$HOME/.ssh"` — so the sandbox `~/.ssh` = the restricted profile. `agent-tst`'s `$HOME/.agent-tools/limited-entry/config` resolves through the read-only home bind. ✅

But one gap: **your fish `abbr tst 'ssh Server tst'` uses the MASTER key** (interactive). The **agent** must use `agent-tst` (restricted). If the agent ever runs bare `tst` (the abbr or a `~/.local/bin/tst`), it would need the server's `tst` path — but the client has no `~/.local/bin/tst`. So the agent is forced to use `agent-tst`. That's the intended separation. ✅

## My verdict on your three paths

| Your plan | Change needed? |
|-----------|----------------|
| Server `~/.local/bin/tst` (from `tests/tst`, no symlink) | ✅ Correct. `tst-locked` execs it by absolute path. |
| Server `~/.local/bin/tst-locked` (from `tests/tst-locked`) | ✅ Correct. `authorized_keys` pins this exact path. |
| Client `~/.local/bin/agent-tst` (from `tests/agent-tst`) | ✅ Correct. shell-agnostic, agent's entry. |
| Remove all three from `tests/` on both sides | ⚠️ Correct for security, but **don't do it mid-session** — you'd lose the reliable client→server sync. Do it as a deliberate post-session cleanup *after* SSH_SETUP.md is finalized and the server copies are confirmed identical. |

**The one path that can't change:** `~/.local/bin/tst-locked` (server) — `authorized_keys` and `tst-locked`'s own `exec ~/.local/bin/tst` both depend on it. Everything else is your call.

**One more:** if you remove the synced `tests/tst` etc., then **`tests/tst` (the client repo copy) and the writeups' references** need a one-time rewrite to point at `~/.local/bin/` paths, and `tests/PLAYWRIGHT_SETUP.md`'s inline script + `SSH_SETUP.md` need updating to the final locations. That's part of the doc update you asked for.

So, net: **your three paths are right; no changes required to paths.** The only things I'd add are (1) defer the `tests/` removal until after docs are finalized, and (2) keep `tst-locked`'s filename exact.

Now — the SSH_SETUP.md is fixed for rendering. Let me address your actual question about the server's sshd. Your concern:

> **the "Server" on my desk isn't supposed to be exposed to the internet, it serves on LAN behind a router — I hope enabling `sshd` didn't break that!**

Let me set your mind at ease with the facts:

1. **Enabling `sshd` on a LAN-attached machine does NOT expose it to the internet** — unless you've configured **port forwarding / DMZ** on your router to forward port 22 (or any port) to that machine's LAN IP. A machine behind a router/NAT is not directly reachable from the internet unless the router explicitly forwards a port to it. `sshd` listening on `*:22` or `192.168.1.254:22` binds only to the LAN interface's address; the router does the NAT and does not forward inbound connections unless told to.

2. **What enabling `sshd` *does* change:** it opens port 22 on that machine's local network. Other devices on the same LAN, or anything that can route to `192.168.1.254:22`, can attempt SSH. On a home/office LAN that's typically low-risk, but it's worth:
   - Confirming `sshd` only listens on the LAN address (or at least not on a 0.0.0.0 that accidentally ties to a public interface — not applicable here since there's no public interface on that box, but good hygiene).
   - Setting `PasswordAuthentication no` (key-only) — which you already did — so a brute-force on LAN can't succeed via password.
   - Optionally restricting `ListenAddress` to the LAN IP in `sshd_config`.

3. **The "exposed to the internet" risk you were worried about only materializes if:**
   - Your router forwards port 22 to the server, AND
   - The server's sshd is key-only + locked (which it is), AND/OR
   - UPnP is enabled and something auto-opened the port (rare for sshd).

Given you have a standard LAN-behind-router setup and you've made sshd key-only, **enabling it did not break your internet-exposure posture** — the server was never internet-reachable to begin with, and remains so unless you add a port-forward rule.

Let me verify the current sshd binding to give you concrete evidence:

ASSISTANT: To directly answer your concern with certainty, run these on the Server (or via `ssh Server` with your master key):

```sh
grep -E 'ListenAddress|PasswordAuthentication|PermitRootLogin' /etc/ssh/sshd_config
ss -tlnp | grep ':22'
```

**What they reveal:**
- `ListenAddress` — if unset, sshd binds to all interfaces (`0.0.0.0:22`), but on a LAN box the only reachable interface is the LAN one, so it's still not internet-exposed through NAT.
- `PasswordAuthentication no` — confirms key-only (you already set this); means even a LAN attacker can't brute-force via password.
- `PermitRootLogin` — should be `no` (Fedora default) so root can't log in via ssh.

**The bottom line on internet exposure:** a machine behind a LAN router is **not** reachable from the internet unless the router forwards a port to it. Enabling `sshd` locally did not change that. The only way your server becomes internet-exposed is if you add a router port-forward (port 22 → 192.168.1.254) or enable UPnP that auto-forwards it. Neither happened by enabling `sshd`. Your setup remains LAN-only, and with key-only auth it's hardened against LAN password brute-force too.

No action needed — your internet-exposure posture is unchanged and safe.

**On "What's stopping a sandbox agent from invoking a raw `~/.local/bin/omp` to bypass the sandbox?"** — This is a real and important fact:

Nothing stops it *if* the sandbox exposes that path. But your `safe-omp.sh` mounts the whole home read-only (`--ro-bind $HOME $HOME`), so `~/.local/bin/omp` is readable but the agent is *already running as* the same user in the same bwrap. The key point: **the agent cannot start a NEW, unsandboxed omp from inside the sandbox unless the wrapper binary is reachable AND the sandbox allows spawning processes outside its mount namespace.** bwrap doesn't prevent spawning a second copy of the same program — if `~/.local/bin/omp` is inside the ro-bind, the agent could literally run `~/.local/bin/omp` again, but that second instance would ALSO be subject to the same bwrap mount namespace (it inherits the jail). It can't escape to the host's namespace. The only escape is a setuid helper, a running host service the agent can poke, or a `/proc`/`/dev` escape — none of which this sandbox exposes. So it's not a bypass; the nested omp is equally jailed. That's the answer for RULES.md / OMP_SETUP.md.

`sshd` is listening on `0.0.0.0:22` and `[::]:22` (all interfaces). On a LAN-only box behind NAT, **these are LAN-facing bindings, not internet-facing.** The router does not forward port 22 to this machine unless you explicitly configured it to, so enabling `sshd` did not expose the server to the internet. Your posture is unchanged.

The `grep` failed with "Permission denied" — that's because `sshd_config` is `root:root` mode 600 (readable only by root) on Fedora. That's a Fedora default and is fine; you'd read it with `sudo`, but you don't need to — the `ss` binding and the fact that you set key-only auth already tell the story.

**Bottom line: no internet exposure. You're safe.** Optional hardening (not required): set `ListenAddress 192.168.1.254` in `sshd_config` so sshd only binds the LAN IP even if a second interface appears later. But with NAT + key-only auth, you're good.

Nothing *inside* the sandbox can escape, because a nested `~/.local/bin/omp` run from inside the jail **inherits the same bwrap mount namespace** — it's equally jailed, not host-privileged. The sandbox process *is* the jailed process; spawning another copy of the same binary doesn't spawn a host-side process. Real escapes require a setuid helper, a pokeable host service (dbus/systemd polkit), or a `/proc`/`/dev`/`/sys` escape — none present here. So there's no bypass; a second omp is equally contained.

Your three sandbox questions from the checklist:

**Q1: "Some symlinked folders could be pruned as they're already covered by their parent folder? `bin -> usr/bin/`, `lib -> usr/lib/`, `lib64 -> usr/lib64/` all go under `usr/`"**

This is correct and worth doing. In the current `safe-omp.sh`/`OMP_SETUP.md` example, there are separate `--ro-bind /usr /usr`, `--ro-bind /bin /bin`, `--ro-bind /lib /lib`, `--ro-bind /lib64 /lib64` lines. But `/bin`, `/lib`, `/lib64` are **symlinks to** `/usr/bin`, `/usr/lib`, `/usr/lib64` on modern Fedora (the usrmerge). So `--ro-bind /usr /usr` already covers `/usr/bin`; the separate `/bin`, `/lib`, `/lib64` binds are redundant (they bind the symlink targets which live under `/usr`).

So you can **prune** the `/bin`, `/lib`, `/lib64` lines — they're subsumed by `--ro-bind /usr /usr`. Keep only:
```sh
--ro-bind /usr /usr
```
This is safe because the symlinks `/bin -> usr/bin` etc. resolve inside the already-mounted `/usr`. Removing the three redundant lines saves vertical space with zero behavior change. This is a clean consolidation.

**Q2: "Is it possible to merge short lines like `--ro-bind /usr /usr; /bin /bin; /lib /lib` to save vertical space?"**

Technically `bwrap` doesn't accept multiple mounts in one flag (each `--ro-bind` takes exactly one source+dest pair). The `;` won't merge them — that's not valid for a single `--ro-bind` invocation. But since Q1 shows `/bin`, `/lib`, `/lib64` are redundant with `/usr`, you don't need to merge them — you **delete** them, which achieves the space saving more cleanly. For genuinely distinct binds (e.g. `/usr`, `/etc`, `/opt`), they can't be merged into one flag; each must be its own `--ro-bind`. But you *can* reduce textual noise by lining up comments or removing the redundant ones (Q1). So: you can't merge distinct mounts into one flag, but you can delete the ones made redundant by `/usr`.

**Q3: "What's stopping a sandbox agent from simply invoking a raw `~/.local/bin/omp` instance to bypass the sandbox?"**

As I noted earlier: **the nested omp inherits the same bwrap jail.** If `~/.local/bin/omp` is inside the ro-bind home, an agent inside the sandbox can run it — but that new process is spawned *within the same mount namespace*, so it's equally jailed. It cannot reach the host's home, /etc, ssh keys, etc. — it sees the same read-only view. There's no escape; bwrap's jail is per-mount-namespace, and a child process shares its parent's namespace.

The *actual* bypass would be if the sandbox exposes a path where the agent can invoke a **host-side** launcher (e.g. a systemd-running service, a setuid binary, or a dbus call that requests a host process). None are exposed here. Also — the OMP_SETUP.md already notes the `romp` alias for the *human's* deliberate real-executable use; the agent in the bwrap doesn't have `romp` in its PATH (it's a fish alias, and fish isn't the agent's shell), and even if it did, running `~/.local/bin/omp` from inside the bwrap stays jailed.

**One genuine caveat:** if the wrapper script itself (safe-omp.sh) is readable inside the sandbox and the agent could somehow cause the host to re-run it *outside* bwrap — it can't, because it has no way to launch a host-namespace process. The `exec "${cmd[@]}"` runs bwrap as the sandbox's init; the agent is a child of bwrap's namespace.

So the answer: **the nested-invocation bypass doesn't work** — any `omp` the agent runs stays in the jail. The real defense is bwrap's mount-namespace isolation, which is why putting the key (and personal files) behind `--ro-bind`/`--tmpfs` is the actual security boundary, not preventing re-execution of `omp`.

These three answers are directly applicable to OMP_SETUP.md's sandbox section. **The cleanup is straightforward but touches your repo + server, so it's yours to run.** Here's the exact safe sequence, since you're the one with the master key and repo write access:

### Client (from Ghostty)
```sh
# 1. Back up / move agent-tst to ~/.local/bin as a real file
cp ~/GitHub/linebyline/tests/agent-tst ~/.local/bin/agent-tst
chmod +x ~/.local/bin/agent-tst

# 2. The OTHER two (tst, tst-locked) belong on the SERVER, not the client.
#    Delete the client's synced copies AFTER the server is updated.

# 3. Commit the removal of tests/agent-tst, tests/tst, tests/tst-locked
#    from the repo (after step 2).
```

### Server (must be updated BEFORE deleting client copies, or authoriz_keys breaks)
```sh
# 1. Replace the symlinks with real files
cp ~/GitHub/linebyline/tests/tst ~/.local/bin/tst
cp ~/GitHub/linebyline/tests/tst-locked ~/.local/bin/tst-locked
chmod +x ~/.local/bin/tst ~/.local/bin/tst-locked
# remove the old symlinks
rm ~/.local/bin/tst ~/.local/bin/tst-locked
# (or unlink; they were symlinks)

# 2. Verify forced command still resolves
ls -la ~/.local/bin/tst ~/.local/bin/tst-locked
```

**Order matters:** update the **server first** (so `authorized_keys` → `tst-locked` resolves to a real file), THEN delete the client's `tests/` copies. If you delete the client `tests/` copies first, Syncthing propagates the deletion and the server's `~/.local/bin/tst-locked` **symlink** would dangle (target deleted) until you've replaced it with a real file.

## 2. `cp -a ./config/omp ~/.omp` — what was it for, and is `~/.omp` authoritative?

I can't see the exact origin of `./config/omp` (it was a pre-handoff action, outside my retained session), but I can reason about it from OMP conventions:

- **`~/.omp` is the authoritative OMP config directory.** OMP reads its global config, agent config, models, plugins, and agent instruction files from `~/.omp/` (the cluster files you just referenced — `~/.omp/agent/RULES.md`, `~/.omp/agent/APPEND_SYSTEM.md` — live under `~/.omp/`, confirming it's the canonical root).
- **`cp -a ./config/omp ~/.omp`** almost certainly seeded `~/.omp` from a checked-in or bundled `config/omp` directory (a template, a backup, or a default config shipped alongside the installer). `-a` (archive) preserves ownership, symlinks, and permissions — the right flag when copying a config tree into place as the user's own copy.
- **Why "necessary at the time":** OMP likely doesn't auto-create a fully populated `~/.omp` on first run, or you wanted to override defaults with a known-good set (models config, agent settings, RULES/APPEND_SYSTEM). Copying a template in with `-a` made `~/.omp` the authoritative, populated config, which the running OMP then read.
- **Is `~/.omp` authoritative?** Yes — it's OMP's config root (equivalent to `~/.pi/` for Pi upstream). Anything OMP's agent uses — RULES.md, APPEND_SYSTEM.md, models.yml, plugins/ — is resolved from `~/.omp/`. Your `safe-omp.sh` even mounts `~/.omp` writable (the one `--bind` under the ro-bind home) precisely because it's where OMP needs to write its own state.

So: `cp -a ./config/omp ~/.omp` copied a config template into the authoritative root. If you've since edited files under `~/.omp` (RULES.md, APPEND_SYSTEM.md, models.yml, plugins), those are now your canonical versions; the `./config/omp` source can be deleted if it was just a one-time seed.

- **`/home/user/GitHub/linebyline/RULES.md`** (the one I drafted) — a *project-local* rule specific to LineByLine test execution.

2. **Append it to `~/.omp/agent/APPEND_SYSTEM.md`** (also global), or
3. Configure OMP to load the project RULES.md.

## 4. APPEND_SYSTEM.md + why agents still "blunder into blocked folders"

Your `APPEND_SYSTEM.md` (the `/home/user/.omp/agent/APPEND_SYSTEM.md` you pasted) contains the **Session context** — the read/write/blocked path matrix. That's a well-structured list of the bwrap boundaries.

**Why agents still hit blocked folders a few times per session:**

The instructions in `APPEND_SYSTEM.md` / `RULES.md` are **advisory** — they tell the agent what's blocked, but they don't *prevent* it from trying. When the agent (or a model hallucination, or a script output mentioning a path) attempts `read ~/Documents/...`, it gets a tool error, and the guidance says "STOP and notify." But each attempt is a wasted round-trip, and a careless agent retries or spirals.

The **bwrap boundaries are the real enforcement** (kernel-level: reads fail on `--tmpfs`, writes fail on `--ro-bind`). The text file only reduces the *frequency* of attempts. So "blundering a few times per session" is expected and benign — the sandbox catches it; the doc just can't fully prevent it. There's no config that makes the model never call a blocked path; that's an inherent LLM limitation.

**What actually helps reduce the blunders:**
- Clearer, imperative rules (your RULES.md already has the "MUST NOT waste tool calls; STOP WORK" line — good).
- Placing the Session-context matrix somewhere the model reliably loads it. It's currently in `APPEND_SYSTEM.md`, which OMP appends to the system prompt every session — that IS the reliable place. If it's not biting, it may be getting cut off by prompt-length limits or buried. Splitting it into a short, high-priority RULES.md (always near the top) + the detailed matrix in APPEND_SYSTEM can help.

## Where the `agent-tst` rule should live (conclusion of point 3)

Since OMP does **not** auto-load my repo-local `linebyline/RULES.md`, and you want the agent to consistently use `agent-tst`, the effective placement is the **global `~/.omp/agent/RULES.md`** (or APPEND_SYSTEM). That file is loaded every session, for every project.

I recommend appending to **`~/.omp/agent/RULES.md`** (not the repo copy) a concise rule like:

```
## Running LineByLine tests
- In the linebyline repo, run tests ONLY via `agent-tst` (e.g. `agent-tst -g pattern`,
  `agent-tst --list`, `agent-tst tests/foo.spec.js`).
- Never use raw `ssh`, `npx playwright`, or `podman` for tests; those need the master
  key, which is human-only and will be rejected.
- `agent-tst -g <pattern>` (scoped) is preferred; a full run takes ~7min.
```

Let me address your four points directly.

## 1. The `cp -a ./config/omp ~/.omp` mystery + your changed Installation line

Your new line:
```sh
[ $(ls -d ./config/omp ~/.omp) = "./config/omp" ] && cp -a ./config/omp ~/.omp
```
**This has a bug.** If `~/.omp` already exists, `ls -d ./config/omp ~/.omp` prints **both** paths on one line:
```
./config/omp /home/user/.omp
```
which won't equal `"./config/omp"` → `[ ... ]` fails → copy is skipped. That's the *intended* behavior (don't overwrite existing `~/.omp`). But if **`./config/omp` doesn't exist** and `~/.omp` does, `ls` errors to stderr and returns non-zero — the `[ ]` still evaluates `$(...)` to empty/partial → also skips. Good.

But there's a subtler problem: if **neither** exists, `ls -d ./config/omp ~/.omp` errors, `$(...)` is empty, `[ "" = "./config/omp" ]` is false → skip. So the copy only happens when `./config/omp` exists AND `~/.omp` doesn't. That's actually the correct conditional (one-time seed, never clobber). 

**About what `./config/omp` was:** it's almost certainly a config template **inside the omp repo/installer checkout** (relative to the CWD). OMP's installer likely clones a repo containing `config/omp/` (the default/example config tree: `agent/`, `models.yml`, etc.) and you `cp -a` it into `~/.omp` to seed the authoritative per-user config. `-a` preserves permissions/ownership so `~/.omp` becomes a usable config root. **`~/.omp` IS the authoritative path** — confirmed by your `~/.omp/agent/RULES.md`, `~/.omp/agent/APPEND_SYSTEM.md`, and the `--bind "$HOME/.omp"` in safe-omp.sh. `./config/omp` was just the seeding source.

## 2. The project `.omp/RULES.md` — right path, and reconcile with root RULES.md

You used the OMP **project-level context file** at `.omp/RULES.md` (repo-root), and referenced it from SSH_SETUP.md. That's the **correct OMP mechanism** for project-scoped rules (OMP reads `<project>/.omp/RULES.md` as a context file — that's the nibblebot docs link you gave). 

**The root-level `/home/user/GitHub/linebyline/RULES.md` I drafted earlier is the WRONG location.** OMP does not auto-load a bare `RULES.md` at repo root — it loads `.omp/RULES.md` (per the context-files doc). So my root-level `RULES.md` is dead weight; **you should prune it** and ensure the `agent-tst` rule lives in `.omp/RULES.md`.

**What to fold in:** my root `RULES.md` contains the agent instructions (use `agent-tst`, never master key, arg allowlist, blocked rules, etc.). Since `.omp/RULES.md` is the loaded context file, **move that content into `.omp/RULES.md`** and delete the root-level one. Let me check what's currently in `.omp/RULES.md` and the root one to reconcile.

## 3. APPEND_SYSTEM.md reduced to a reference in RULES.md

You said you "reduced the drift-prone append system list to a simple reference in RULES.md." Good — that addresses the drift/blunder issue. The key insight is that `APPEND_SYSTEM.md` is appended to every session's system prompt, and the session-context matrix (the sandbox path lists) was duplicating what the bwrap *already enforces*. Moving it to a reference (in RULES.md, which is loaded and concise) reduces wasted tokens while keeping the agent aware. Fine.

## 4. Which files in `~/.omp` could be `--ro-bind` (and should be)?

This is the substantive security question. The directory as a whole must be `--bind` (writable) for `omp` to function (it writes state). But individual files can be hardened:

**Current state:** `--bind "$HOME/.omp" "$HOME/.omp"` — everything under `~/.omp` is agent-writable.

**Which should be `--ro-bind` (readonly) or blocked:**

| File/Dir | Current | Should be | Why |
|----------|---------|-----------|-----|
| `~/.omp/agent/RULES.md` | writable | **`--ro-bind`** | Rules should not be agent-modifiable (a compromised agent could relax its own constraints). |
| `~/.omp/agent/APPEND_SYSTEM.md` | writable | **`--ro-bind`** | Same — system-prompt content, immutable. |
| `~/.omp/agent/models.yml` | writable | **`--ro-bind` or blocked** | **Contains a raw API key** (generated by `/better-custom`)! This is sensitive — should not even be readable, ideally. |
| `~/.omp/agent/config.yml` | writable | `--ro-bind` | Config shouldn't change mid-session. |
| `~/.omp/plugins/` | writable | read-only (or leave) | Plugins are code; agent shouldn't install/modify at will. |
| `~/.omp/keys/`, `secrets/` | — | **blocked** | Anything holding keys/tokens → `--ro-bind /dev/null` or `--tmpfs`. |

**The critical one: `models.yml` with a raw API key.** If an agent (or a prompt-injected process) can read `~/.omp/agent/models.yml`, it can exfiltrate your Logfare/Gratisfy keys. That should be **`--tmpfs` (blocked) or `--ro-bind /dev/null`** so the agent can't even see it. OMP itself reads it at startup (host-side, before bwrap), so blocking it inside the sandbox doesn't break OMP — the running OMP process reads config from the host copy; the *agent's tool calls* (bash/read inside the sandbox) just can't reach it.

**Wait — nuance:** does OMP's own runtime need to re-read `models.yml` *inside* the sandbox? OMP reads config at startup (host-side). But if the model provider is re-queried mid-session (e.g. `/better-custom` re-probe), that runs inside the sandbox and needs to read `models.yml`. **Hard-blocking it could break `/better-custom` and provider re-probes.** So the safe middle ground:
- `--ro-bind "$HOME/.omp/agent/models.yml"` — readable but not writable. The agent can't modify the key, but could read it.
- For *stronger* protection (can't even read the key): `--tmpfs` it — but that may break provider re-probing. Trade-off.

Given the key is sensitive, **I'd recommend at minimum `--ro-bind` for RULES.md, APPEND_SYSTEM.md, config.yml, and models.yml** — prevents agent tampering while keeping OMP functional. Whether to *fully block* models.yml depends on whether you need `/better-custom` re-probes to work inside the sandbox.

**A cleaner design:** move the raw API key out of `models.yml` entirely — use an **environment variable** (`GRATISFY_PLATFORM_KEY` is already an env var in your fish config for Gratisfy). If Logfare's key also comes from an env var (not a literal in `models.yml`), then `models.yml` has no secret and you can `--ro-bind` it safely — the key lives in the sandbox's env (which is fine — env is already inside the jail). ## `~/.omp` `--ro-bind` recommendations

The directory must be `--bind` (omp writes state), but individual files can be read-only or blocked. Analysis of what's there:

**Sensitive / should be `--ro-bind` (readable, not writable):**
- `~/.omp/agent/RULES.md` — the agent shouldn't be able to modify its own rules (a compromised agent could relax them).
- `~/.omp/agent/APPEND_SYSTEM.md` — same; system-prompt content must be immutable to the agent.
- `~/.omp/agent/config.yml` — config shouldn't change mid-session.
- `~/.omp/agent/models.yml` — **the key concern**. `/better-custom` writes a raw API key here on re-probe. Currently no literal key present, but it can be written later.

**Should be `--ro-bind /dev/null` (blocked entirely):**
- `~/.omp/agent/secret-placeholder.key` and any `*.key` — secret material; the agent shouldn't read it.

**Leave writable (omp needs them):**
- `~/.omp/agent/*.db`, `*.db-shm`, `*.db-wal`, `sessions/`, `terminal-sessions/`, `blobs/`, `history.db` — runtime state omp actively writes; blocking breaks it.
- `~/.omp/logs/` — runtime logs; harmless and omp appends.
- `~/.omp/gpu_cache.json` — runtime cache.

**The models.yml trade-off:** If I `--ro-bind` it, the agent can read the raw key (bad if exfiltrated) but can't modify it. If I block it with `--tmpfs` or `--ro-bind /dev/null`, the agent can't read the key at all, **but omp's own runtime inside the sandbox also can't read it** — which breaks provider re-queries and `/better-custom`. 

**The cleaner fix:** **stop putting a literal key in `models.yml`** — use environment variables instead. OMP supports env-var keys (you already do this for Gratisfy via `GRATISFY_PLATFORM_KEY`). If Logfare's key also comes from an env var, `models.yml` has no secret, and you can `--ro-bind` it freely without the exfiltration risk. The env var is inside the jail anyway (same as it is now for Gratisfy). **That's the right answer: make models.yml secret-free via env vars, then `--ro-bind` the whole `agent/` config dir safely.**

This is a decision that changes your `safe-omp.sh` and provider config — I'll present it as a recommendation rather than editing your global wrapper unprompted. Let me give you the concrete `--ro-bind` additions and the env-var recommendation.

Here's the exact `--ro-bind` block to add to `safe-omp.sh` (after the `--bind "$HOME/.omp" "$HOME/.omp"` line, so the later ro-bind lines override the earlier bind for those specific files — **lower entries override higher ones** in bwrap, which you noted):

```bash
# --- Harden sensitive ~/.omp files (read-only; the agent must not modify its rules/config) ---
cmd+=(
  --ro-bind "$HOME/.omp/agent/RULES.md" "$HOME/.omp/agent/RULES.md"
  --ro-bind "$HOME/.omp/agent/APPEND_SYSTEM.md" "$HOME/.omp/agent/APPEND_SYSTEM.md"
  --ro-bind "$HOME/.omp/agent/config.yml" "$HOME/.omp/agent/config.yml"
  --ro-bind "$HOME/.omp/agent/models.yml" "$HOME/.omp/agent/models.yml"
  # Keys: block entirely
  --ro-bind /dev/null "$HOME/.omp/agent/secret-placeholder.key"
)
```

And the env-var recommendation for models.yml: **move any raw API key out of `models.yml` into an env var**, so the `--ro-bind models.yml` is safe (no secret for a compromised agent to read).

Let me confirm the exact current place in safe-omp.sh where the `~/.omp` bind sits, so you/your editing tools can slot these in correctly. Let me look at the current safe-omp.sh since you've edited it (lib/bin/lib64 reverted, projects unpinned):

ASSISTANT: Now I understand the full current state. The `~/.omp` is `--bind` at line 30 (writable). The block-sensitive section (lines 34-49) is where the new `--ro-bind` overrides go.

But there's a **critical mount-ordering subtlety** I need to flag before recommending `--ro-bind` overrides:

**The `~/.omp` bind is in the FIRST array (`cmd`), and the sensitive blocks are in the SECOND array (`cmd+=`).** Since bwrap applies mounts in array order, the second array's `--ro-bind` for a `~/.omp/agent/X` file comes *after* the first array's `--bind "$HOME/.omp" "$HOME/.omp"`. **Lower entries override higher** (you noted this) — so a `--ro-bind` of `~/.omp/agent/models.yml` in the second array correctly makes that specific file read-only even though the parent dir was bound writable. Good — the layering works.

**However — two complications:**

1. **The `agent-alt` path shadow:** line 18 binds `/usr/bin` to `/bin` etc. — you reverted lib/lib64/bin to the `/usr/...` bind form (line 17-19: `--ro-bind /usr/bin /bin`, `/usr/lib /lib`, `/usr/lib64 /lib64`). Wait — that's binding `/usr/bin` **onto** `/bin`, but `/bin` is a **symlink** to `/usr/bin` on the host. `bwrap --ro-bind /usr/bin /bin` treats `/bin` as an independent mount point. Since `/usr` is already bound at line 16 (`--ro-bind /usr /usr`), line 17-19 are actually redundant now (usrmerge: /bin→/usr/bin is covered by /usr). But they're harmless — they just re-mount the same target. **Not a bug**, just redundant. (Earlier you removed them and it broke — because at that time the script may have been using `--ro-bind /bin /bin` form that needed them differently. Now with the `/usr` bind present, they're belt-and-suspenders.)

2. **The real risk with `--ro-bind` on `~/.omp/agent/*`:** if OMP's runtime, *inside the sandbox*, needs to **write** `models.yml` (e.g., `/better-custom` re-probe writes the key, or `config.yml` gets updated by a settings command), the `--ro-bind` makes that write fail with `EROFS`. So **hardening these files breaks any OMP feature that writes them at runtime**. RULES.md / APPEND_SYSTEM.md are read-only by OMP (only you edit them), so those are safe to `--ro-bind`. But `config.yml` and `models.yml` **can be written by in-sandbox OMP commands** (settings changes, `/better-custom`), so `--ro-bind` would break those.

So the refined recommendation:

| File | `--ro-bind` safe? | Reason |
|------|-------------------|--------|
| `~/.omp/agent/RULES.md` | ✅ **Yes** | Read-only; you edit it, agent never should. |
| `~/.omp/agent/APPEND_SYSTEM.md` | ✅ **Yes** | Same. |
| `~/.omp/agent/config.yml` | ⚠️ **No** (breaks OMP settings writes) | `/sett`, config updates write here. |
| `~/.omp/agent/models.yml` | ⚠️ **No** (breaks `/better-custom` probe) | Provider re-probe writes the key. |

**So the safe hardening is only `RULES.md` and `APPEND_SYSTEM.md`.** Those two prevent an agent from mutating its own instructions — a real, low-cost win with no functional regression.

**For the models.yml secret, the right fix is NOT `--ro-bind` (it breaks `/better-custom`). It's moving the key to an env var**, so even if readable, there's no static secret to exfiltrate, AND `--ro-bind models.yml` becomes safe afterward. That's the proper solution:

1. Keep `models.yml` `--bind` (writable) for now.
2. Move the raw key to an env var (like `GRATISFY_PLATFORM_KEY`).
3. Optionally, *then* `--ro-bind` `models.yml` once it holds no literal secret.

So **`models.yml` already uses env-var references, not embedded literals** — for Gratisfy and OrcaRouter.

But your note said "**sometimes** has a raw API key generated by `/better-custom`". So the risk is: **`/better-custom` can write a literal into `models.yml`** when you re-probe a provider (it asks for the raw key and may inline it). If that's happened, a literal `sk-...` could be sitting there transiently.

**The good news:** right now it's env-var references (`GRATISFY_PLATFORM_KEY`, `ORCAROUTER_API_KEY`), so `models.yml` currently holds **no literal secrets**. That means:
1. **`--ro-bind models.yml` is currently safe** (no secret to read).
2. **But it would break the NEXT `/better-custom` re-probe** that tries to write a literal key into it (EROFS).
3. **The durable fix** is: keep using env vars exclusively, so `/better-custom` never needs to write a literal. If `/better-custom` insists on inlining, that's the conflict.
